# The Return of Zero

Frank G. Taylor

A note on citations and standing. The apparatus of this working candidate is uneven by section, and the unevenness is stated rather than hidden: some sections carry anchored notes, §1 collects its notes at the section's close without in-text markers, and other sections cite in the prose itself, by name, edition and locator. Capitalised *Argued*, *Derived* and *Offered* are the essay's own claim-status terms — a position argued across domains on reasoned grounds, a position derived in the essay's native field, and a conjecture offered for trial. Contemporary technology and institutional material is held at the resolution of developer reports and documented programmes, pending verification in the essay's source bank. Several quotations ship wording-verified against their source records, with print collation still pending; where that is so, the quotation's standing is wording-verified, not print-collated.

## §0/1 — The Integral Threshold

What knows?

Somehow every attempt at defining this slips through humanity's fingers. Every description of "the I" falls short insofar as it “knows”. We can study the brain, recount a life, reconstruct or deconstruct a self-image, even follow the steps by which one says "this or that is so, for so-and-so", and what we know seems to grow. But follow any thread faithfully and you'll have to reach to the moon and back - any persistently inquisitive toddler inevitably embarrasses the adults in the room, who may well have forgotten that asking for an account of one thing entails an account of everything; suddenly its bed time, or some other excuse materialises such that the adults can avoid the looming sense of their entire sense of reality becoming relativised, and thus dwarfed, on the spot. Humanity, however, does quest into this grand game of "gathering and speaking out", we are not simply thinking-beings, we are questioning and questing being. As our accounts of reality grow denser and more deeply stratified, the demand necessarily returns to the one who makes it; it's a wonder why this isn't the first place we seek such definition, but I guess a large portion of life for anyone is spent wrestling with the questions of I, You and Us. Completeness of knowledge cannot sidestep the region in which the account settles. Our questions return with their answers, emboldened; and what humanity has found thus far hasn't settled the questioner. In truth, it seems the more we can describe and define the world around us in objective terms and formal systems the more urgently and pointedly the question of identity rises to disquiet us. To ask "what knows" is altogether a different class of question to asking "who knows". The conflation of the "who" and "what" of identity, and how this conflation is implicated in our collective, epochal disquiet, we will unpack through this work. 
<!-- movement:M01 (continuation) -->

The parent can bring the conversation to an end more easily than bring the inquiry to a conclusion. A set law, bedtime, is the reason, not just supplied but *enforced*, which settles what happens next. The child may need sleep, the adult may be exhausted, and a decision has to be made; but the decision also reveals something about the answers that preceded it. They were being given by someone with a concern, from within a world in which certain reasons already counted. Another “why?” can make that concern apparent. “Because I say so” is the point at which the person giving the account steps into it as its authority, its arbiter.

Much of what the child is questioning was received by the parent in turn. The words in which an explanation is offered, the distinction between a good reason and an impertinent question, the expectations surrounding work, sleep and obedience belong to a life already being lived before this evening’s conversation began. The parent can explain some of that inheritance. In doing so, another part of it becomes visible: perhaps a remembered instruction, perhaps an anxiety about tomorrow, perhaps a conviction whose origin has never been examined. A question about one thing has begun to disclose the world through which the answerer knows it.

The child is learning more than the answers. There is also what an answer is allowed to settle, whom one asks, when another question is welcome, and when asking threatens the relation that makes an answer possible. These lessons can enter the child’s later thinking as a voice no longer recognised as borrowed. An inherited “because” comes to feel like the way things simply are. To question it then is to encounter something intimate: a habit of mind through which the world has acquired its familiar shape. The dependence was active long before it became a subject of reflection.

Trust begins within that dependence. The child can ask because a language and a relation already bear the asking; the parent can answer because memory, experience and other people’s testimony already support the reply. Neither has first certified the whole arrangement. Inquiry takes up this received confidence and puts particular claims at risk. The unsettling question is valuable because it can disclose a reliance that had been doing its work unnoticed, and let that reliance become more carefully understood.
A parent participates in bringing forth a life and helps form the world through which that life will recognise itself. The child comes to speak in words the parent supplied, then uses those words to ask a question the parent cannot answer. Here the relation between creator and created turns back upon its origin. What has been given form acquires the capacity to address the one who helped form it. Their difference makes the address possible; their common language lets it reach. The parent’s role changes in the encounter, too: the giver of explanations becomes someone whose explanations are being examined.

Creator and created consequently name positions within a relation that neither party surveys from outside. The parent was a child; the language belongs to other speakers; the life brought forth enters circumstances its parents did not arrange. To account for creation is already to follow a dependence beyond the immediate maker. To encounter what has been created is to discover what the account of its making has left unsettled. The metaphysical question of source and manifestation begins to acquire its ordinary force here. An origin becomes intelligible through a life whose unfolding exceeds the intention that welcomed it; the one brought forth can return a question to the one who brought it forth.

Artificial agency makes this formative relation available to deliberate construction. In Agentworld, Benjamin Bratton describes an agent taking shape through active context: trained capacities become a particular course of activity through the situation in which they are brought to bear. Persona is one layer among memory, skills, constraints and relations. What the system can receive, retain, pursue and do participates in the formation of the agent that acts. An account of its intelligence must therefore concern the world through which that intelligence becomes operative.[^s01-bratton-formation]

This changes the object of technical work. Give an agent access to a source it could not previously consult, alter what it retains from an encounter, or change the actions it is permitted to take: the next judgment proceeds through different conditions. A source is more than another item awaiting quotation when access to it changes what can be asked; a remembered correction matters when it changes how a later answer is reached. The means of knowing enter the constitution of the next act. By constructing and revising those means, we begin to work technologically upon what this essay calls Objective Internality.

The term lets us ask a common question across different instances of mind: through what internally related conditions does a world become available for discrimination and action? In a human life, a remembered rebuke can shape the hearing of an innocent question. In a constructed agent, retained instructions and prior exchanges can shape the processing of a new request. In each case, something received has become a condition of receiving again. Its internality lies in that participation. A rule need not be hidden inside a skull or a machine to govern what happens through it; a name, a promise, a document or another’s permission can belong to the operative world from which an act proceeds.

Bratton gives the return its most economical statement: “First, agents model humans, and then become the model against which humans model themselves.”[^s01-reversal] The created model enters the creator’s subsequent self-understanding. Imagine an agent helping someone describe their abilities, organise their work or decide which aspirations are realistic. Its account can become material through which the person recognises and directs themselves. Later interactions receive the person partly formed by those earlier descriptions. What began as a model of a life is now participating in the life’s way of modelling itself.

The parent–child scene lets us recognise the direction of this return. The child’s question can change the adult’s understanding of what it is to be a parent; the artificial interlocutor can change the human practices from which its own construction arose. Making an intelligence places the maker inside a relation whose consequences return. Our definitions of helpfulness, competence and success help determine what we build; what we build can then help determine how we understand those definitions. The inquiry becomes self-referential at the level of its conditions.

Power enters through the capacity to shape those conditions. The parent decides when the questioning ends. The designer determines which requests reach a system and which responses receive reinforcement. Each decision gives a developing intelligence some of the circumstances through which it will distinguish an answer from an error, an opportunity from a prohibition, itself from what it can act upon. Those who make such decisions act from circumstances of their own. The question therefore returns through the maker: which inherited assumptions, desires and images of intelligence are being passed on, and what can the ensuing encounter change in them?

A more complete account can disclose more of this activity. We can follow how a judgment was formed, what it excluded, and how its result altered the next situation. Yet the activity through which we follow it is itself thinking, discriminating and relating. Giving an account of the instrument brings the instrument into view; knowing that account raises again the question of what knows. We have reached the distinction that governs the whole inquiry: the means through which something becomes known can themselves become known, while the one for whom they appear remains to be understood.

The child’s name gives us a place to begin. Someone chose it, someone received it, and a life has grown around its use. The name lets the parent call the child back into the room. Eventually the child can ask what the name means, why it was chosen, and whether it says who they are. Long before any of those questions could be spoken, however, there was already someone to whom the name would be given.

<!-- movement:M02 — the theorem -->

A definition gives something a determinate place. It distinguishes what a term applies to, excludes alternatives, lets consequences follow; without that boundary we could not tell whether we had answered a question or changed the subject. Aimed at the Subject, this power turns on the one wielding it: whatever is to be distinguished must be distinguished while the distinguishing is going on. Refusing every determination would leave both the question and its examiner unlocated. So a definition will be made — made seriously, with a claim committed to it — and what it does will be watched.

Before the parents agree on a name, they can already disagree about what to call this child. The referent of their disagreement has not waited for the agreement. One of them can lift the unnamed baby, pass the baby to the other, and recognise the same child being returned. Their attention has held one life distinct through a change of place and of hands. Here is the count beneath naming: this one, and this one again. They need recite no numeral to distinguish the one who receives the name from another who could receive it.

Giving a name carries this identification into further encounters. People absent from the first scene can use it; records can retain it; its bearer can answer to it. But another child may receive the same name, and this child may later choose a different one. The sound can remain while the bearer differs, or change while the bearer remains identifiable. The name works through a relation which repetition of its sound cannot supply by itself.

Saul Kripke gives that relation a precise modal articulation in Naming and Necessity. A proper name rigidly designates the same individual across the counterfactual situations under consideration, whereas most descriptions can select different individuals. His preface distinguishes establishing which use of a name is intended from asking what could have happened to its bearer. Shared sounds do not undo rigidity: each reading is considered separately. His account of transmission follows names through speakers who intend to retain the reference they received.[^s01-kripke]

Our inquiry follows what this practice already has to accomplish. This one distinguishes an instance; another admits a second; this one again recognises a return. Counting, in this elementary sense, holds instances apart and holds an instance through different appearances. It is prior to the recitation of numbers, and prior to the particular name by which an instance is made publicly addressable. Naming then changes the means available for keeping that identification: what was pointed to can be spoken of, remembered and encountered through others.

The unnamed baby makes this priority visible without removing the relation in which it occurs. A count is being made from somewhere, by someone, with something held in view. The child in a parent’s arms, the child in yesterday’s photograph and the adult imagined in a possible future enter different circumstances of identification. Calling each by one name does not make a body, a photograph and an expectation interchangeable. We understand what is being identified through the relation between them.

Name, instance and circumstance thus divide the work. The name articulates; the instance holds what is being identified; the circumstance makes the relevant sameness and difference intelligible. “That is our child” can identify a figure in a photograph while acknowledging that the photograph is not the child. “That could be our child” can concern a possible future without confusing it with a present fact. The small changes in the sentence alter what is being claimed while the shared reference allows us to understand what the alterations concern.

The parents have been relying on this ordering before explaining it. Their recognition holds the child, the image and the possible future in their different places within one intelligible situation. Here intuition has a definite task: to keep that whole available while a particular distinction is made within it. The explicit account can then examine how the identification was sustained and where it may have gone wrong. What was tacitly held becomes articulable through its own differentiation.

Kripke’s two dice make the distinction countable. Keeping the dice identified as A and B gives thirty-six ordered outcomes; ignoring which die bears which value gives twenty-one unordered possibilities.[^s01-kripke-dice] The outcomes vary while the identity of each die is retained. In our own elementary case,

\[
(A=1,\ B=2)\qquad\text{and}\qquad(A=2,\ B=1)
\]

are different assignments with the same total. The difference between them disappears from an account that records only the sum. Nothing mysterious has happened: the account has changed what it retains. The total answers one question exactly, while the distribution answers another. Which distinctions are kept determines what the resulting number can tell us.

This is why counting cannot be detached from the circumstance in which something counts. Two people with one name are two people, while two names may concern one person; a changed description may tell us more about the same life, or reveal that we had confused two lives. The act of identification must discriminate these possibilities. Its accuracy consists in the relation it maintains, including the differences required to maintain it.

Ordinary speech carries much of this work in is. “The child is tired” predicates a condition; “this is the child we were speaking of” identifies the one whose condition concerns us. Tomorrow’s child can be rested without our losing the person to whom yesterday’s tiredness belonged. In grammar, the joining word is the copula. Its philosophical pressure comes from the double task already performed in the encounter: something must stand forth in a determination, and the determination must remain attributable to what has been encountered.

Even “A is A” makes a minimal journey. The second inscription is not the first, yet the judgment identifies them. If we remove their difference of placement, we lose the two presentations between which the identification was stated. If we remove their common identity, we lose the reason for identifying them. The equation

\[
3=1+2
\]

holds two differently composed expressions equal in a definite respect. Their numerical equality remains exact while their written forms remain different. The equals sign allows the difference to become intelligible as a relation of identity.
There is a further insistence in the simplest addition. In $1+1$, the unit is the same unit-kind twice, and its two occurrences must remain distinguishable for the count to increase. The recurrence of one makes two possible. Thus identity and difference are already working together inside number: the count needs the sameness through which its units belong together and the difference through which there is more than one. This is the numerical face of the problem we encountered in naming. The person changes; the changes concern that person. A sign repeats; the repetition concerns that sign.

Self-knowledge intensifies the requirement. There must be something of myself available to recognise, hence a difference between the present act and what it encounters. Yet the recognition must concern myself if it is to be self-knowledge. The two demands belong to one undertaking. The demand on our account of the Real is already active here: identity has to remain itself through differences that genuinely occur. In the intimate case, this becomes the question of how I can encounter myself in a different presentation and recognise that difference as mine.

We can write the copula’s work with the simplest marks. A dash placed here and another placed there are the same kind of mark in two positions. Between them, an oblique stroke makes their spacing explicit:

\[
-/-.
\]

At this point the dashes carry no assigned numerical values; we are reading the composition of a mark. Gather the two parallel strokes and we have $=$, the sign through which distinguishable expressions are held equal. Retain the oblique stroke $/$ as cut or differentiation. Crossing the first sign with the second produces $\ne$. The sign for non-equality visibly contains the sign for equality under the cut that changes its force. Two operative marks have composed a third sign.

Their composition lets us inspect the relation already argued. To identify something, it must be distinguishable; to distinguish it, there must be enough identity for the distinction to concern this. Identity appears through difference, while difference is itself identified. Neither operation can complete the work alone. We write $=/\ne$ to hold their mutual implication in view: equality related to non-equality through the stroke that both separates and joins them.

The stroke has two readings. OR allows either side to be considered in its distinct office: identity as what is maintained, difference as what makes articulation possible. AND holds their dependence together within the act. Identity is articulated through difference; identity also differs from difference, since something must remain identifiable through its articulation. Following either reading gives us a determinate account. Following their relation requires that the account retain both.
The copula can now receive an inscription of its own:

\[
/ = \bigl(\; = \quad =/\ne \quad \ne \;\bigr).
\]

The first term inside the brackets gives identity, the last difference, and the middle exposes each to the other. Read that middle from either end and it gives two faces: identity in its relation to difference, and difference in its relation to identity. With the outer poles, we have four appearances. The $=$ and $/$ through which they are composed remain active throughout. Four articulated appearances, two composing operations:

\[
4+2.
\]

The account includes the acts by which its terms became available. We began with distinguishing and identifying, followed their involvement in one another, and arrived at a field composed through those same operations. The brackets gather that field; the outer equals sign identifies its whole activity with the slash. The writing is doing what it says: it distinguishes a relation, unfolds its composition and identifies the resulting whole with what it set out to understand.

Even the count can return upon itself. Hold the middle $=/\ne$ as one relation instead of counting its two faces separately, and the four appear as three terms held in a context. Their containing act supplies the fourth office. Thus $4$ can be read as $3+1$ without discarding either face of the middle; what changes is where the account locates their unity. The two composing operations remain. A relation has given an account of its own articulation, and the accounting has become a further enactment of that relation. The definition of the Subject must now face this same demand. Its account has to include the identifying activity through which the account is made.

The child who received a name enters this work from a position the name itself cannot speak. Kripke explicitly includes “I” and “you” among rigid designators, even though their referents vary with the context of utterance.[^s01-kripke-persons] The child says I where the parent says you. When the child addresses the parent, the positions reverse. Each can also be spoken about. The encounter therefore admits first-person, second-person and third-person accounts while preserving the different places from which they are made.

Those places have consequences. A parent can say “you are frightened”; the child can answer “I am angry.” The answer can correct the description while retaining the one it concerns. More is happening than a change of predicate. The person who was being spoken about has spoken from within the life under description. The parent’s account now has to meet an address, and the shared language that made the initial judgment possible also makes its correction possible. An identification becomes answerable through the difference it has crossed.

The name can tell us whom we are discussing. Descriptions can tell us what that person has done, remembers, expects or feels. Yet when I say I, the reference also enters from the side of the present speaking. A complete account of the circumstances would itself have to be understood through an act occurring somewhere within them. The movement towards the Subject begins when the identifying activity, so far directed towards its objects, becomes the question it is trying to answer.
“I see these words” seems to offer three terms for inspection: I, seeing, words. The words can be inspected. The seeing can be investigated—eye, attention, language, the circumstances of reading—and the findings can make its operation more exact. “I am aware of seeing these words” opens the sentence further: the seeing has become something known, a process that seemed to supply the knower’s identity now a presentation to the knower. An account of that process is another presentation. A self-portrait can be faithful; a second can include the artist making the first; a third, the artist studying both. The additions disclose more of the activity and its circumstances, and none takes the place of the present seeing in which the portraits are encountered. Even a portrait of the viewer is a portrait for a viewer.

The point has a history, a true advent relative to Modernity's burgeoning Mind. In 1641 René Descartes undertook to withhold assent from whatever could be doubted: the senses, which had deceived him; the world before him, which could be a dream; even arithmetic, if a deceiver of unlimited power wished him deceived. The doubt is performed, and it stops at its own performance. Let him be deceived as much as can be imagined — the being deceived is still there, occurring, if only as doubt. What he then does with the finding is a second move. He asks what this I is and determines it: a thinking thing, whose activities include understanding, affirming, denying, willing, imagining, sensing.[^s01-descartes] The certainty of the occurrence and the further account of what occurs are different achievements. An account of thought can be questioned while the questioning is still given; the occurrence held fast in a way the determination of the doubter never could. For this inquiry the sentence has to be opened: I am aware of thinking. Thought is the means — the determinate activity through which something is grasped; awareness is the fact in which the activity appears; and the I is exposed not as a conclusion proven by thinking but as the condition in which thinking, proving and doubting can be given at all. This is reached only by an explicit recursive move, following Descartes, passing back over the mind's condition to the fact beneath, belief's origin; this is an instance of what we'll develop as *epi-logos*. Notice the register of Descartes' inquiry; he had to face up to the primacy of trust. 

That presence was there before the reflection began. Watching a thought changes my relation to the thought; it does not manufacture the knowing in which the change is apparent — otherwise the watcher would need a watcher before anything could be known, and every beginning would postpone itself again. The correction matters because a natural misreading lies that way: that each deepening reflection produces a new, finer knower behind the last. What Lacan and Derrida saw in language as *glissement* and *difference* manifests too in the quest for the Subject, which slides beneath each object purporting to capture it, be it neural correlate, pneuma or noun. What each reflection produces is a new describable relation. Pratyabhijna, what may be translated as *recognition*, when it comes, is a change of the mode of identification, not just another change in the image. The images I hold of myself remain active while their claims to being "the I that knows the image" are relinquished.[^s01-recognition]

The relation between knowing and known can be tested from both directions at once. Make them wholly indistinguishable, and the difference through which anything can be known disappears: nothing would be presented to anything. Sever them completely, and what is known could never reach the knower: presentation without an address. Each attempt to totalise the relation leads equally to pathology, destroying a condition of the occurrence it meant to explain, the limitation of a posited and assumed duality. This is because the knower by necessity is the *principle* of subjectivity, subjectivity as such or what we call Subjective Immediacy; naturally thought begins at the objective pole, yet logically we must impute and accept its opposite because *we are here to*. Subjectivity *as such* cannot be any one or other instance of *a* subject, as simply by the implicit count in the instance such a subject is already part-object, a spectral form stretching the difference. This stretch, where objects are *taken as subjective forms* and equally where the Subject *assumes this or that object*, is what we term Objective Internality, commonly and flexibly termed "Mind", the means by which any object can appear as such to some-one, and such activity appears as available, in some world, for others. Knowing requires a unity that simultaneously bears the difference between what can be taken as distinct and what remains only something we can gesture at, or symbolise, precisely because it anchors all possible knowledge in an irreducible "!/?". What appears belongs to the occurrence of knowing, while its particular content does not exhaust the presence to which it appears. The spoken returns to whereof one cannot speak; its the "one must" that makes "be silent" salient. 

What knows, however, cannot be settled by more knowing of the same kind, for even at face value the demand to represent the Subject *as* an object is effacing of their necessary mutual difference, and thus precludes any possible attestation to their identity. The identity of knower, means of knowledge and known, which anchors their relation, provides a clue to why: everything that can be named or counted seemingly emanates from the demand of the uncountable to still be accounted for, the *desire* of the subjective pole towards form (we'll encounter this notion in registers spanning religious ontology to depth psychology to politics and sociology). An accounting of the Subject cannot, despite the yearning instinct of thinking, to make "a thing" of itself. The Subject is not absent from the attempt at definition; it is the attempt's own condition, present before, during and after as the one undergoing it. I pinpoint the issue at the general tendency of thought to reify what is quite viscerally and immediately a processual reality. A definitive act about such a condition can only be performed, because a coherent object of knowledge is not necessarily an invariant one. Truth here is not stored in a crèche of certainty, awaiting the correct sentence to collect it, but rather the attempt at true-saying is enacted, and enacted necessarily, lest we make "a thing of it" - it seems language itself has conspired to brim these paths with dead ends, brambles and cul-de-sacs.  Any definition of the Subject must accordingly try and fail at the impossible. The failure is not only a defect of wording, it is the formal limit of the rational laid bare — failing exactly where actual present Being and the capacity to experience reside. The argument is given this early, in so raw a form, because it carries the logic of everything downstream.

The definition, before it runs, is setup as follows. *The Subject is the whole that any coherent representational system participates in, depends upon and necessarily fails to contain*. "Whole" as a term is fixed as it is spoken: the whole approached in self-presence, from the side of knowing, resting on what Jung may have called "psychic facticity", that all phenomenal experience is factually real from the perspective of Subjective Immediacy. A person's present world appears within that circumstantial, existential given. Every *self* is thus whole, yet insofar as a mind is constituted by objects of experience known presently *and* exactly everything absent from the present account, wholeness remains impossible for the embodied one, in the world. Knower, means of knowledge and known must keep their distinct offices inside the one relation that identifies them, which puts the relation under pressure precisely because "the means" are so often collapsed into "the knower, and thus mind holds that it does, or even could, find wholeness. Lacan's notion of desire raises its head here, as the notion of "constitutive lack" can be seen as a necessary condition of the mind. The tendency to collapse in exactly this way is what the unfolding of this initial setup seeks to address, concretely. But to "get it" we have to "get there", so our walk must begin elsewhere; we must begin with the proposition that *The Subject is that which cannnot be defined at all*.

The Tetralemma or Catuṣkoṭi, the canonical Madhyamaka Buddhist count of anything sayable will be our guide here. Simply it amounts to 4 possible offices of assertion; it IS; it IS NOT; it BOTH IS AND IS NOT; it NEITHER IS NOR IS NOT. This four cornered logic was developed to exhaust the capacity of assertive proposition, such that "the real" (a notion thats itself necessarily *bracketed in the inquiry*) could acquire determination through and beyond determinations of or about it. For now, let's *use* the Tetralemma actively as a guide for our definition, and run our definition through its geometry.

First corner: it IS. The Subject is that which cannot be defined. The sentence seems almost perfectly fitted to its object because it refuses to place anything positive where the Subject should be. No quality is assigned, no image installed, no substance smuggled in. The definition keeps the ground clear by saying only that every determination must miss.

Second corner: it IS NOT. The refusal has already done what it forbids. “That which cannot be defined” is itself a determination: it marks a boundary, supplies a predicate, gives the Subject a place in speech. The sentence has formed the formless and handed the result back under the name of its impossibility. Its failure is exact. In trying not to make an object of the Subject, it has made an object called that which cannot be made an object.

Third corner: it BOTH IS AND IS NOT. Now the contradiction begins to say something. As a representation the definition fails; as an act it succeeds. The phrase cannot contain the Subject, yet the failure occurs presently, for someone, and genuinely discloses the limit it names. What seemed a defect in the sentence becomes its content. The Subject both is and is not that which cannot be defined: is, in the refusal enacted; is not, in the determination thereby produced. The paradox does not ask to be solved by choosing a side. It is the form of a relation whose terms cease to make sense when required to stand independently.

Fourth corner: it NEITHER IS NOR IS NOT. Push the contradiction to exhaustion and neither verdict remains sufficient. “Is” and “is not” were positions occupied in an act of determination, and the act has now disclosed something their content could not: there was a position from which the positions were being taken. The sentence has not captured its Subject. It has exposed the one for whom capture was a concern.

The tetralemma has exhausted assertion, but our count is not yet complete, because the one walking its four corners has not been counted.

The first further movement is recognition. I tried to know myself by producing things that could be known—names, thoughts, images, definitions—and each became another presentation before me. Their succession has not manufactured the presence to which they appear. The one defining was implicated before the first proposition was uttered. The subject of the proposition was already the Subject of the act. I am.

The second movement is return. Recognition does not abolish the world of determinations. The person who says I am continues to think, desire, speak, answer to a name, mistake one thing for another and change his mind. What returns is discrimination with the limit now included in it. The definition has failed to possess the Subject and thereby acquired a determinate truth about Mind: whatever appears does so through a difference between that which can be presented and that to which presentation occurs. Form appears; its appearing cannot itself be exhausted by another form. 

Jung entered this same predicament not initially as a logical exercise but as a struggle of mind, desire and life. In The Red Book he describes turning desire away from things and men, only to find himself captured by his thoughts. The desert opens when even this identification fails: the self he seeks is “neither in events, nor in men, nor in his thoughts.” What thought ordinarily treats as its own interior turns out to contain objects like any other—memories, fantasies, propositions, desires, images—while the occurrence in which they appear remains uncaptured by their enumeration. 

The distinction can now be made cleanly. Indian epistemology names within an act of knowing pramātṛ, the knower; pramāṇa, the means of knowing; and prameya, the known. Thought, perception, memory, language and representation belong to the middle office: they mediate what can become known. They are not thereby identical with the knower. A thought can be watched. A desire can be resisted. An image of oneself can be altered. The recursive movement adds another pramāṇa, another means by which something is presented; it does not manufacture another Subject behind the last.

In the vocabulary of this inquiry, Subjective Immediacy names the knower, Objective Internality the means, and World the known. Life, or Mind in its fullest sense, is the whole within which these offices become distinguishable. The middle term includes the mind through which I remember, imagine, understand and act; calling it objective internality makes explicit that even my most intimate thought can become an object of attention. Its intimacy does not remove its form. Nor does recognising its form deprive it of life. The same thought that can be examined also changes what I notice, what I want, and what I do next. 

Kashmiri Śaiva philosophy approaches this living relation through prakāśa and vimarśa: luminous appearing and its inherent power of self-apprehension. In Dyczkowski’s exposition, a mirror can display an image without knowing that it displays; awareness includes the knowing of its manifestation. Requiring another awareness to illuminate the first would begin an endless postponement. Reflexivity belongs within the occurrence. Through it, awareness differentiates a world, remembers what has passed, imagines what has not occurred, and directs itself toward an act. Will, knowledge and action articulate this power in different ways. The non-dual here is alive in the distinction: what appears and the power through which it appears belong to one self-articulating whole. 

This gives formal language to the difficulty Jung lived. Desire goes out toward things, people and thoughts because each offers a form in which the self might be found. Yet every achieved form appears in turn as something known. The search therefore slides. Its torment is not accidental: the means continually offers itself in the place of the knower.

“I will be myself once I have this.” The desired object acquires a task no object can complete. An achievement, a lover’s recognition, an explanation of the universe, even an image of one’s own enlightenment can become the thing through which existence is supposed finally to justify itself. Desire gives the form its urgency; identification lets the form speak for the one desiring. When everything else must answer to it, the form has become an idol. The question of what becomes one’s god begins here, in the authority granted to an image.

Power operates in this passage. A description can help me understand myself; it can also become the measure against which I am permitted to exist. Another person’s description of me can do the same. Naming and predication therefore act within the gap they cannot close: they organise a life through determinations that never possess its first-person occurrence. Recognition returns that power to its proper work. I can use a name, inhabit a role, pursue an end and remain answerable for what follows, while allowing the life concerned to exceed the form through which I have understood it.  

Jung knew how readily this border receives the name madness. Plato had already made the word unstable. In the Phaedrus, Socrates distinguishes madness arising from human affliction from a divine mania through which prophecy, initiation, poetry and love become possible. Internet Classics Archive Jung takes up that inheritance. Delusion can occur when the depths overwhelm a person until he identifies himself with them; it can also occur when the “spirit of this time” permits him to see only the surface and to take that partial order for the whole.

Here an ordinary cultural insult hides a logical question. Mad, irrational, nonsense: these words can name genuine disintegration, but they can also be used superstitiously, as charms against whatever exceeds an accepted means of determination. Reason becomes unreasonable when it mistakes its own instrument for the extent of the Real. The answer is neither the abandonment of discrimination nor capitulation to every voice from the depths. It is better discrimination: knower, means and known held in their distinct offices without being torn out of the one occurrence in which knowing happens.

Hence Jung’s language around the Word. Coming out of the desert he calls the word a creative act and says that the oldest and truest words “oscillate between nonsense and supreme meaning.” Later the symbol itself occupies this threshold: it cannot simply be fabricated or discovered as an already finished object; it becomes. Meaning here is not a dead packet carried by an arbitrary mark. The symbolic form allows a relation that could not be held by either isolated term to come into expression. 

This is the point at which our own notation has to be born.

The old Greek symbolon gives the image its body. A token could be broken between two parties, each keeping a half; brought together later, the break itself authenticated the relation. Difference was not an obstacle to recognition but its condition. One piece alone could not perform what the two accomplished by fitting across their division. 

Call the elementary form of that relation 0/1.

\(0\) names the pole that cannot enter its own count as one object among others: Subjective Immediacy, the unobjectifiable condition of appearing. \(1\) names determination: the first formed, counted, identifiable something. The stroke is the relation by which they can be spoken together without being collapsed. It divides and joins at once. The primordial symbolon is therefore not zero beside one but their broken unity: distinction as the condition of relation, relation as the condition of identity.

The two marks already carry two senses of one. Zero bears the singular One, self-identity; one bears the manifest All, self-difference. Any identifiable something belongs to this second office, because its being this already involves what distinguishes it, what sustains it, and the circumstance in which it can be identified. A first mark opens a field of possible marks. The All is present in that relational constitution before anyone finishes an inventory. Thus \(0\) cannot become one item alongside its manifestations, while \(1\), however singular its appearance, arrives through multiplicity. 

Counting performs this distinction in its simplest acts. In \(1+1\), the same unit must be available twice, and its two occurrences must remain distinguishable. Without the sameness, there is no common unit; without the difference, there is no two. Even a collection counted as one holds a multitude under one identification. Mathematics continually works this relation between unity and multiplicity, identity and its repeated or transformed instances. The earlier \(A=A\) has acquired a count: the second \(A\) can be identified with the first precisely because the inscriptions remain two.

Metaphysically, this is the predicament of the One and Many. How can the Real be wholly itself through differences that are themselves real? The distinction between self-identity and self-difference allows both requirements to stand. Being does not become a fraction of being when it appears as this being; this being nevertheless has its definite limits. Mono and poly name these two offices. The difficulties that later gather around monotheism and polytheism already press within them: how the One becomes imageable, how many images participate in it, and what happens when one image claims the source as its exclusive possession. 

An old image makes that non-possession visible enough to think with. The Book of the Twenty-Four Philosophers, an anonymous medieval gathering, gives as its second definition of God an infinite sphere whose centre is everywhere and whose circumference is nowhere. Nicholas of Cusa receives and transforms the image in On Learned Ignorance. An ordinary sphere gives its centre a unique position and stations a boundary beyond every interior. The image withdraws both at once: access to the centre cannot be monopolised at one place, and the enclosing edge cannot be stationed past the whole. Presence is intimate without becoming private property.    

Read through \(0/1\), the centre is the unobjectifiable presence from which a world appears, and the circumference is the articulated horizon through which that presence becomes encounterable. Every situated life supplies an entrance; none supplies the perimeter of the Real. The image therefore brings the paradox into view: wholly here, without the whole becoming enclosed here. What no finite picture can successfully display has nevertheless generated a picture whose operation can be understood. The definition’s predicament has begun to give itself a symbolic definition.

We will call such an inscription a matheme. Lacan introduced the term for formal symbolic constructions capable of carrying relations with a precision ordinary exposition repeatedly has to unpack. Their written structure was meant to make those relations transmissible beyond the originating speaker. The term is useful here for the same economy. A matheme is not a replacement for argument. It is an argument brought into a form that can itself be inspected, transformed and returned to. 

So turn the symbolon over:

\[
\frac{0}{1}\quad\longrightarrow\quad\frac{1}{0}.
\]

Ordinary arithmetic supplies the resistance we require. \(0/1\) presents no difficulty as a quotient; \(1/0\) cannot be assigned an ordinary numerical value. The inversion makes the limit visible inside mathematical notation itself. What was allowed in one orientation becomes impossible in the other. Rather than hiding that impossibility, we use it. The matheme asks what relation has been preserved across a transformation whose numerical reading has broken down.

The orientations can now be read from within the defining act. In \(0/1\), the condition becomes articulate through a determination; in \(1/0\), that determination turns toward the condition it cannot turn into another determinate possession. The slash carries both readings—AND, because they belong together; OR, because either orientation can be taken and their difference remains consequential. Holding the readings together produces

\[
\frac{0/1}{1/0},
\]

the relation taking its own inverse as a term of further relation. Its superposition is explicit: the two orientations remain available together, including the difference between what arithmetic determines and what it leaves undefined. 
Wolfgang Pauli pursued this capacity of mathematical signs in his essay “Modern Examples of ‘Background Physics’,” preserved with the Jung correspondence. Reflecting upon the imaginary unit \(i=\sqrt{-1}\), he observed that its introduction could give mathematical theorems a simple and distinct form; for Pauli, mathematical representation was “a symbolic description par excellence.” He immediately extends the point to mathematics’ qualitative work, naming number theory and topology. Our use is deliberately elementary: where the quantitative reading reaches its prohibition, the inscription remains available as a symbolic relation. 

This does not take us away from the rational. It catches rational determination in the act of generating its own boundary. The non-differentiable can be named only by differentiating it from the differentiable; the non-dual becomes a term only by entering a duality with what it is said not to be. Language performs the cut it denies. The contradiction is therefore not verbal debris to be swept aside. It is evidence of the predicament being stated.
Count the predicament now.

The tetralemma supplied four explicit positions: is; is not; both; neither. Recognition then gathered their performance back to the one who had enacted them; return restored that one to determination without forgetting the limit. Four corners and two turns:
\[
4+2=6.
\]

The same count can be generated within the minimal inscription. There are two single terms, \(0\) and \(1\). Relating this binary to itself produces four ordered pairings: \(00,\ 01,\ 10,\ 11\). Retain the single terms together with their two-place possibilities, and the result is

\[
\underbrace{0,\ 1}_{2}
\qquad
\underbrace{00,\ 01,\ 10,\ 11}_{2^2}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
2+2^2=4+2.
\]

The count distinguishes the terms from the ways they can enter their own relation. The two becomes six when its original distinction and its self-application are held together. The defining act did this in another material: four determinate positions, together with the recognition and return through which those positions became one self-related performance. 

Give those four predicative positions a boundary: the four sides of a square. Each side can now be related to its opposite by identification, the topological operation through which distinct representatives are taken as one in a newly constituted space. Identify the left edge with the right and the bottom edge with the top, matching their directions. Joining the first pair makes a cylinder; joining its remaining circular ends makes a torus. Its surface is generated by what has been identified across the cuts. Pi Mathematica
The boundary records two directions, each encountered forward and in reverse:

\[
a\,b\,a^{-1}\,b^{-1}.
\]

Four boundary-occurrences; two generating relations. After identification, \(a\) and \(b\) become the torus’s two basic loops, one winding around the tube and the other around its central opening. More generally, a closed orientable surface of positive genus \(g\)—a surface with \(g\) handles—has a standard presentation by a \(4g\)-sided polygon whose paired edges yield \(2g\) generating loops. For the torus, \(g=1\):

\[
\underbrace{4g}_{\text{polygon sides}}
+
\underbrace{2g}_{\text{generating loops}}
\quad\xrightarrow{\ g=1\ }\quad
4+2.
\]

This accounting holds the articulated boundary together with the relations through which it becomes a surface capable of return. The four and the two give different readings of one construction: its explicit presentation and its generating circulations. Pi Mathematica 
The identifying relation can also be written across an indefinitely extended plane:

\[
\mathbb T^2=\mathbb R^2/\mathbb Z^2,
\qquad
(x,y)\sim(x+m,y+n),
\quad m,n\in\mathbb Z.
\]

Here the slash performs a quotient: points separated by whole-number translations in either direction represent the same point on the torus. An infinite lattice of representatives acquires one finite surface-address. The many and the one are joined by the rule of identification; the rule itself tells us how to pass between them. The copula has become constructive. This counts as that makes a space. 

A path makes the consequence tangible. Begin at \((0,0)\) on the covering plane and travel to \((1,0)\). On the torus, the endpoints coincide: the path has returned to its starting point. On the plane, the displacement remains. Both descriptions are exact. Return has identified the endpoints while retaining the journey’s winding, so the loop is distinguishable from having stayed still. The torus’s two independent winding counts give

\[
\pi_1(\mathbb T^2)\cong\mathbb Z\times\mathbb Z.
\]

The notation records how a path goes around the surface; returning to the same place does not erase how it returned. Here the elementary \(A=A\) has acquired motion. Identity is maintained through a difference which its identifying relation can carry. 
Recognition and return now have an image in which to move. Recognition gathers a differentiated traversal as one act: I can identify the one who arrives with the one who set out. Return brings that achieved identification back through the condition of its possibility. The surface’s opening enters the account through the circuits it makes possible. Filling it would alter those circuits and the identity of the surface itself. What cannot be occupied as another patch of surface is nevertheless determinate in its consequences for movement.

Within Quaternal Logic, or QL, the four explicit positions are numbered \(1\) through \(4\); recognition occupies \(5\), and the implicate condition through which the traversal renews is \(0\). The numerals here locate offices in the cycle:

\[
1\longrightarrow2\longrightarrow3\longrightarrow4
\longrightarrow5\longrightarrow0.
\]

The two implicate offices receive their topological expression through the two independent circulations: gathering the articulated field as a whole, and returning that whole through its relation to the opening it cannot fill. Thus \(5\rightarrow0\) names the turn by which achieved recognition returns to its ground. The cycle completes through what enables it to begin again.  

Cusa’s image and the toroidal construction can now work together. The centre everywhere and circumference nowhere gives the whole in the intimacy of each situated entrance; the torus lets us follow a determination returning through the relations that gave it form. The first exposes what a finished picture cannot enclose. The second makes that non-enclosure active as a way of going. The Subject’s resistance to definition has generated an account of its own defining, and that account has generated a form which returns through the limit it accounts for.

This is the surprise inside the attempted definition. The more faithfully it encounters its impossibility, the more exactly it acquires a symbolic body. The statement could not settle the Subject as an object; its performance has nevertheless become intelligible as distinction, self-relation, identification and return. The act of defining the Subject counted itself into six.

The definer belongs to the definition; the walker belongs to the walk; the counting activity appears inside what has been counted. What began as a question of definition has become a problem of self-reference. Its emerging signature can now be followed through three elementary enactments: the proposition returning upon its own assertion, the binary retaining its own pairings, and the polygon becoming a returning surface through identification. In each, the operation takes its own terms back into account and changes the form in which they can be understood.

Here is the wager of the work: a self-referential act generates a form that can be recognised across its different enactments, and the recognition of that form participates in the same act. The formula refers back to the activity by which it is written and read. This gives the later Meta-Epistemic Framework its starting point. Different means of knowing refract the relation differently; returning through those means makes their participation available to inquiry. The present movement supplies a first worked instance of the cross-domain recognition that §4 will develop. 

Power remains inside this return. A local determination can carry the whole’s relation without becoming proprietor of the whole. The same distinction holds between a name and its bearer, a self-image and the one living through it, a god-image and what gives that image its authority. The symbolic form remains fertile while it lets the relation pass through it. It becomes an idol when the passage is arrested and the form requires everything to terminate in itself. What becomes one’s god is therefore inseparable from what one allows to govern the next act of identification. 

And self-reference will not remain a philosophical curiosity. A system able to represent the world may eventually represent its own representing; a system able to alter the world may become one of the objects it alters. In the contemporary discussion of recursive self-improvement, AI research itself enters this loop: systems participate in building and evaluating subsequent systems. Dario Amodei’s phrase “pacing the frontier” names the concern that this recursion can advance faster than the understanding and control brought to bear upon it. The technical question returns us to the formal one: when the actor enters its own field of action, what holds the distinction between knower, means and known? 

We need not answer that question here. It is enough to notice why alignment becomes urgent at this horizon. Human beings have never lacked the ability to act against what they know, to mistake a representation for the whole, or to identify themselves with powers that pass through them. The possibility that our artefacts acquire deeper self-reference does not introduce the problem so much as return it to us in another form.

The present theorem is smaller and more foundational. A definition of the Subject must attempt the impossible because the Subject is implicated in the attempt itself. Its failure yields a distinction; the distinction yields a relation; the relation yields a count; and the count, recognising its own counter, yields the first matheme. Its successive forms can now be read as transformations of the same act:

\[
\boxed{\quad
0/1
\;=\;
1/0
\;=\;
4+2
\;=\;
5\rightarrow0
\quad}
\]

The relation appears from either pole, unfolds through its own self-relation, and returns through the condition it cannot enclose. The equal signs carry identification across those differences. What the copula first required, the matheme now performs. 
Sense and nonsense now oscillate inside the inscription. The impossibility is not outside the logic. It is what makes the next logic necessary.

The definition has not captured its Subject. It has done something more useful: it has given the Subject’s impossibility of capture a form in which that impossibility can think.

<!-- movement:M03 — definition as cut, gift, danger -->


When the Subject’s definition returns us to the one who defines, it also changes what we can hear in the ordinary claim, “I know myself.” The sentence distinguishes the one who knows from the self made available to knowledge, while claiming that the two belong together. Everything turns upon this belonging. I can recognise myself through a description, remember myself through an image, or understand something of my life through another person’s words. Each gives me something definite to work with. The trouble begins when that definiteness seems to settle what I am, and the means through which I have come to know myself quietly assumes the authority of the one who knows.

Our language carries both sides of this predicament. Behind the Latin *scire*, to know, lies the reconstructed root *skei-*, to cut or split, also found in *scindere*. *Scientia*, knowledge, gives us *science*. Knowing cuts: it distinguishes a voice from the surrounding noise, a remembered event from an imagined one, an explanation from an excuse. The last distinction already requires more than an inventory of words, since the same sentence can serve either purpose. We have to understand what the speaker is doing, what occasioned the statement, and what acknowledging it would change. The cut becomes more exact as more of the relation becomes intelligible. 

But *scire* also enters *conscire*: knowing together, knowing with. From this family comes *conscientia*, whose history joins shared knowledge, inward awareness and moral testimony. The familiar separation between **consciousness** as awareness and **conscience** as moral awareness took shape chiefly in the seventeenth century, as philosophers increasingly distinguished an awareness of thinking from an evaluation of what one thought or did. Latin *conscientia* and French *conscience* carried senses which modern English would distribute between the two words. The distinction we now hear so readily had to be worked out within a vocabulary in which being aware and being answerable remained intimately entangled. 

At the end of the sixteenth century, the English theologian William Perkins gives the *with* a distinctly inward depth. In his *Discourse of Conscience* of 1596, *conscire* names two parties knowing a secret together. Another person can know what I reveal, but God knows even what I conceal; conscience, Perkins argues, makes the person a participant in that divine knowledge of their own life. It can speak against the person or on their behalf. Its testimony is therefore more intimate than an accusation arriving from outside, and potentially more unsettling: what I say about myself is already exposed to a knowing which my account does not command.

Augustine had given this inward exposure its confessional form much earlier. In Book X of the *Confessions*, he addresses the God to whom the depths of conscience are open even when the speaker refuses to disclose them. He also recognises that a person does not wholly know themselves: something of the life remains obscure to its own spirit, while God knows it entire. The confession consequently includes what Augustine knows of himself and what he does not. He is already addressed, already known, before he can finish the account by which he would know himself.

Here **knowing-with reaches what is not-known**. The theological language gives an inward witness to what exceeds the person’s self-description; our earlier definition makes its formal pressure explicit. God cannot be located as one more observer somewhere inside the experience, for any such observer would have become another figure appearing within it. The Subject remains intimate to the knowing through which that figure could be encountered at all, but this knowing is unconditioned, not an "I know x" but "I know that I know". Withness is structurally and existentially the nature of this being witness to your own witnessing-being. I can testify to what I did, discover what I concealed from myself, and recognise a responsibility that my preferred description had left out. In the language of conscience, God is this knowing that like air becomes implicit and silent in the life I am trying to tell. The knowing accompanies its expression while exceeding what the expression makes explicit: the witness is present through the telling, yet the telling can return the speaker to a withness they had forgotten in their account of themselves - if one can "be silent", or rather, "let silence be".

f witnessing occurs through “you” or “me” as its aperture, then the particularity of a life carries a responsibility already implicit in its knowing. Through these eyes, this history, these habits of attention, something of the world becomes available to understanding and action. I can discover a motive I had concealed from myself, but I can also recognise how my attachment to a particular account has governed what I was willing to see. The old intimacy of consciousness and conscience becomes intelligible here: the life is witnessed from within, and the person giving its account is answerable for how they have participated in its disclosure. “I knew what I was doing” acknowledges this participation; “I did not want to know” can reveal it more painfully, because I recognise that I helped maintain the ignorance through which I acted.

The ego-self claims sovereignty when it treats this aperture as its possession and appropriates the knowing that occurs through it. My account then speaks as though its authority originated in me: what I admit becomes what is real, what I exclude becomes what need not concern me. The tyranny begins intimately, in the demand that a life conform to the image through which I have understood it, and extends outward when others must conform to that image too. Yet the witnessing which gives my experience its immediacy is equally present through theirs. Their suffering cannot become unreal because my description has found no place for it. Conscience can interrupt this appropriation by returning me to the withness of witnessing: I am implicated in what becomes visible through this life, while the knowing itself exceeds my claim upon it. Letting silence be allows that excess to reach me before I have furnished it with an answer. I may then have to change how I look, what I acknowledge, and what I do to the person whose life my explanation had kept at a distance.

Hobbes, writing in *Leviathan* in 1651, still uses *conscious* for people who know a fact together and can therefore testify to it. But he also notices the manoeuvre by which someone lends the respected name of conscience to a cherished opinion and then demands that the opinion be treated as inviolable. Shared testimony and private self-certification have come to occupy the same word. A parent and child both know that a promise was made. They may disagree about what it now requires, but that disagreement concerns something they already hold between them. When the parent’s conviction of being right is allowed to determine what the promise must have meant, the common knowing has been subordinated to one participant’s account of it.

English offers us a less reverent pun at precisely this point. The *con-* of knowing-together can become a **con**: the knower is persuaded that the image in which it recognises itself is what it finally is. Mono, the unqualified One, is taken in by a determination within Poly, the field of its appearances. “I am this sort of person” begins as an intelligible account of habits, attachments and circumstances. Eventually the account may decide which experiences can be admitted as mine, which desires are permissible, which memories must be explained away. The picture has become the authority before which the life must justify itself.

We can write the concealment directly:

\[
0/1\;\xrightarrow{\;\text{the }0/\text{ is occluded}\;}\;1.
\]

The arrow follows a change in the account. The relation continues to operate: something is still appearing, being recognised, desired, defended. But the determinate self-image now presents itself as the source of that activity. Its acquired terms inherit the intimacy of *I am*. A child who has learned to be “the clever one,” “the difficult one” or “the good one” may eventually experience a challenge to the description as a threat to being someone at all. What was received as a way of understanding life has become the condition under which life is permitted to understand itself. The later crossed-zero account will follow this concealment through its successive recognitions. 

To see how a distinction acquires such power, we must first let it do its ordinary work. A boundary gives *here* by also giving *there*. Inside and outside arise through the same line; each lets us locate the other. This is the finite face of the geometry we approached through Cusa’s reception of the infinite sphere. There the centre could not be monopolised and the circumference could not be placed around the whole. An ordinary circle, by contrast, gives a definite enclosure within an already available space. We can point to its edge, measure its interior, cross from one side to the other. Its exactness consists in making a local difference, and that difference immediately gives us two related places from which to proceed.

In his letter to Jarig Jelles of 2 June 1674, Spinoza calls this work of finite determination *negation*. A body has a figure through the limits of its extension: its outline determines the shape by marking where that shape ends. “Determination is negation” therefore makes the boundary productive. The circle becomes this figure because its extension is bounded in this way. The line gives us something measurable and, at the same time, distinguishes the extension beyond it. What looks like the positive possession of a shape has been achieved through a relation to what that shape excludes. 

Among Indian Buddhist philosophers, the corresponding difficulty arose within an inquiry into reliable knowledge: how perception, inference and language can reach what they claim to know. Dignāga brought particular pressure to the difference between encountering a particular and understanding it under a general concept. We have followed a child’s proper name through the changes of one life. The word *child*, however, applies to many particular lives. What permits one word to gather them? And how can it do so if the universal apparently named by the word is not itself a separately existing thing shared among its instances? His theory of *apoha*, exclusion, answers by examining what the act of conceptual identification rules out. 

To apprehend something as a cow is, in this account, to exclude what is other than a cow: **cow is articulated as not-non-cow**. The apparent simplicity of the positive term conceals this double work. *Non-cow* opens the field from which the term distinguishes its application; excluding that otherness allows these differing particulars to be understood together. Their conceptual likeness is accomplished through the distinctions the term permits. The animal in front of us can differ in size, colour and countless other respects from one encountered yesterday, while those differences are set aside in the act of recognising both as cows. What the word gathers depends upon which differences it treats as relevant.

The formulation asks us to examine how a positive meaning is constituted. Simply cancelling the two written negatives would return us to the word *cow* while leaving that work unexplained. The distinction between forming an exclusion-term and denying a predication matters here: “non-cow” helps specify the range of a concept, while “this is not a cow” makes a judgment about its application. Our traversal of the tetralemma has already made us attentive to what an assertion does when affirmed, denied, held with its denial or displaced by neither verdict. Apoha brings that attention into the apparently uncomplicated act of saying *this is one of those*. The affirmative judgment carries a negative condition of its own intelligibility. 

The textual detail reaches us through Ole Holten Pind’s critical study and translation of the fifth chapter of Dignāga’s *Pramāṇasamuccaya*, his compendium on the means of knowledge. Pind reconstructs the argument through its surviving textual witnesses; a passage he translates, provisionally attributed to another work of Dignāga, describes the referent as a thing qualified by preclusion rather than mere preclusion. This keeps something important in view. The act of exclusion makes reference possible; it has not left us with an empty collection of negatives in place of the thing encountered. A real particular is reached through a conceptual distinction whose generality depends upon what it excludes. 

What if we now ask for the meaning of the terms through which that distinction was explained? We find ourselves using further terms, and those, too, acquire their sense through relations to others. Jacques Derrida gives this movement the name **différance**, allowing the French verb’s senses of differing and deferring to act together. A sign is distinguishable through its differences from other signs; its meaning also refers onward, through contexts and expressions which the present mark cannot make entirely present at once. What is absent leaves a trace within what is understood - this is what lets the child ask "but why" to ever answer the parent supplies. Spacing and delay belong to the production of meaning itself, as much as the referential nature of teh part to the whole. Even the silent difference between the *a* of *différance* and the *e* of *différence* makes the written distinction consequential where their spoken forms give it no separate sound.

The parent’s promise gives the temporal aspect an ordinary life. “I will listen” reaches toward an encounter still to come. What counts as listening will be tested by what the child later says and how the parent receives it; that encounter can also change the understanding of what was promised. The meaning is already effective as it can reassure the child, create an expectation, make another question possible, while its further determination remains exposed to an event neither party has yet lived. We can make the promise clearer, but no final wording performs all its future keeping.

In psychoanalysis, Jacques Lacan describes the **glissement**, the sliding of the signified beneath the signifier. The signifier is the articulating mark or word; the signified is the meaning which takes shape through its relations to other signifiers. What slides is therefore the signification, rather than a physical referent wandering out from under its name. A sentence can retrospectively change how its earlier words are understood, and a signifying chain can gather around a *point de capiton*, a quilting point through which its meaning acquires a particular hold. Lacan locates the efficacy in the chain and its articulations: an isolated element does not contain the entire signification it can come to bear. 

Jung’s movement between meaning and absurdity can now be followed through the moments at which a signifying chain settles into an intelligible account. What had seemed scattered acquires a relation; something makes sense, and a course of action becomes possible. Yet Jung calls meaning “a moment and a transition”: what gathers a life under one understanding can subsequently become the obstacle through which another understanding must pass. The quilting point gives this passage a temporary place to stand. Once protecting my child has acquired a definite meaning, I can decide what protection requires; the same words can lead me to receive a difficult disclosure or prevent it from being spoken. The practical difference lies in how their meaning has been fixed.

In the case of an artificial agent, fixing the meaning of a task likewise narrows the range of possible actions. This is useful precisely where it is dangerous. Suppose an agent settles upon an interpretation of an argument and begins searching for its justification. A supporting quotation now has a definite place in the developing account; a plausible passage can fill that place so convincingly that the work of establishing its source is displaced by the satisfaction of finding that it fits. Hallucination can inhabit meaning’s phantom form: the generated statement fulfils a real semantic role while the event, source or authority it purports to report has been supplied by the account itself. If the passage is subsequently treated as evidence, its fit directs further reasoning and action, and those later steps can make the initial fabrication increasingly difficult to dislodge. As an interpretation settles, it begins to organise what the agent will seek, accept and do. Learned dispositions, instructions and the available context enter the judgment; through their encounter, the agent produces the particular determination under which its next act proceeds. What counts as coherence is itself at stake: the fabricated quotation may fit the argument because the interpretation has already decided what a fitting source should say. The gathering of meanings into an account has begun to govern the gathering of evidence. Here Logos approaches nomos: an intelligible arrangement acquires the force of a rule, distributing what may count and what may follow.

For someone trying to become “a good parent,” the quilting can extend through a whole life. *Good* gathers memories of one’s own parents, inherited expectations, fears of failure, images of affection, rules about discipline. An objection from the child reaches into this arrangement. It may change what good parenting is understood to require; it may instead be heard as a threat to the identity under which all those commitments have been gathered. The predicate’s meaning is bound to other predicates, while its authority has become bound to the person’s sense of being themselves. Here the sliding of meanings and the rigidity of identification can sustain one another: the standard changes whenever necessary to preserve the claim that I already embody it. The greek term oikos, the household, gives this ordering its daily life - it is also at the root of the word "economy". Time, attention, work and permission are apportioned among its members; a parent’s understanding of goodness helps decide who receives what and what each owes the others. This is the practical depth of nomos: a meaning becomes a custom, a custom guides a decision, and the decision changes how the household’s members can live together. At political scale, a disagreement over what a policy would do can become a test of who belongs with, or to, whom. The proposed measure is judged by the colours it wears; what should have been a question about the common life becomes a contest in defending the side through which one recognises oneself. Red against blue: the law emerges as sporting, rather than as ubiquitous supporting.

Lacan’s Name-of-the-Father brings the authority of this arrangement into view. The paternal function receives its symbolic force through the relation between a person and the law they represent. A parent saying “because I am your father” invokes more than an individual preference: the title calls upon an order of kinship, obligation and authority through which the instruction is supposed to have standing. The child receives both the instruction and a place within that order. The parent thus administers an order through which they, too, became a parent; claiming its authority also binds them to obligations toward the child. Lacan’s distinction lets us ask what happens when the person embodying the function treats their own wishes as identical with the law that gives the function its force. A tyrant makes this appropriation in his own person; under what Sheldon Wolin calls inverted totalitarianism, it can be sustained through an order whose public leaders remain replaceable. Corporate ownership, political finance and privileged access to policymaking concentrate power through institutions that continue to speak in the name of popular sovereignty. The elected representative gives that power a face, while the interests shaping the office’s available choices remain much harder for the electorate to remove. Authority can therefore survive the public repudiation of the person who exercised it.  A representative can consequently serve as both agent and scapegoat, appearing as a 0 from below, as a 1 from above. They come to represent total consequentiality for political decisions, despite the utterly undeniable interconnection between political, financial and industrial interests. An officeholder may be only partially and proximately responsible for genuine harm and still become the point at which the investigation of responsibility is made to stop. When their disavowal or dismissal is presented as the restoration of public sovereignty, the interests that financed, constrained or profited from their decisions can escape the same reckoning. The sacrifice of the representative protects the arrangement they represented.
