Derrida's openness can be practised in the same way, as attention to what an expression carries beyond the meaning we have fixed on it, and Wittgenstein's later account of rule-following states the matching practical requirement. Knowing how to go on cannot be secured by adding an interpretation to every application, because each interpretation must itself be understood and applied, so there has to be a way of grasping a rule that is exhibited in following it.[^s01-pi] We need ways of learning, correcting and acting through which a received expression can meet a circumstance it did not specify in advance, and a language for intelligence must support that capacity, or its fluency becomes one more means of defending the interpretation it began with.

Descartes's scientific inheritance carries the same question to the scale of a civilisation. His *Meditations* began from the certainty of experienced thinking while the existence of material things remained under examination, and in the Sixth Meditation Descartes insisted that he was not in his body as a sailor is in a ship, observing its damage from outside: pain, hunger and thirst disclosed a lived union.[^s01-descartes-sailor] An inheritance that keeps his distinction between mind and matter, treats matter as independently certain, and then asks whether experience can be reconstructed from it has reversed the order of his inquiry, and calling that order "Cartesian" hides what was selected and what was discarded. It keeps the thinking subject as a methodological point from which the world is represented while stripping its first-person certainty of the standing Descartes gave it, and the resulting world-picture appears able to explain the knower from whose act of knowing the investigation proceeded. Adam Frank, Marcelo Gleiser and Evan Thompson call this reversal the blind spot of science.[^s01-blindspot] Their diagnosis places lived experience, the presence of perception and its first-person character, at the point scientific materialism relies on and fails to acknowledge as fundamental, and they separate the achievements of science from the philosophical commitments of objectivism and physicalism: a scientific model is obtained and tested through experience, while the further claim that physical reality exhausts everything is an ontological commitment, not one more experimental result. Our argument places the Subject at exactly that spot. Whatever an investigation presents as the knower becomes another determinate object, while the occurrence of knowing remains involved in presenting it, so the omission is inevitable in any account that admits only objects to its explanatory terms. An investigator can describe neural events, reported experiences and the relations between them; the structural question concerns the knowing in which those descriptions are available, including the knowing by which an explanation is recognised as successful.

Scientific materialism postpones that question by appealing to the completeness of a future physics. It admits the present account to be unfinished while granting the promised account, in advance, the power to explain everything that presently exceeds it, and the authors of *The Blind Spot* point out that if "physical" means whatever a future complete science will describe, its content has not yet been supplied; the expectation nevertheless governs what counts as a serious explanation now. The God-image's former office survives the rejection of its theological name. An ultimate account is expected to arrive and justify the terms under which inquiry already proceeds, so that the faith lies in the promised totality and the concealment lies in presenting that promise as the absence of metaphysics. Russell's request returns here as a culture's: investigation is asked to supply the basis of faith, and the absolute limit is treated as an approachable one not yet approached. A researcher can sincerely believe they have relinquished every such commitment while applying a criterion that admits only one kind of final answer, and the self-misrecognition is inward as well as public, because the confidence needed to pursue a programme is mistaken for knowledge that its ontology must prevail.

David Chalmers's concept of the **hard problem of consciousness** states the consequence as a precise distinction.[^s01-chalmers] Explaining discrimination, integration, report or behavioural control does not by itself explain why these functions are accompanied by experience, and a complete functional account can leave that question exactly where it found it. Neural correlates bring real relations between experience and physiology within reach of investigation; expecting the Subject to appear among them at last repeats the objectification our definition has already undergone, offering another identifiable process in place of what knows the process. What the governing description excludes comes back as a problem the description cannot stop generating, an epistemic return of the repressed, and here the shadow of the scientific self-image shows plainly: an inquiry devoted to evidence grows defensive toward the first-person occurrence through which evidence has any standing, because granting that occurrence a foundational role would unsettle the image of explanation to which the inquiry has become attached. Cosmology meets the observer by another door, for Brandon Carter's anthropic principle requires our expectations about the universe to take account of where and when observers like ourselves could exist, since an observation selected by the possibility of observing is not an unrestricted sample from everywhere and everywhen.[^s01-carter] We can make those conditions ever more explicit, and their explicitness does not place us outside them, because the cosmological account is made from within the universe whose history it describes. In both cases the investigation is strengthened when it states its position and distorted when a situated account borrows the authority of an imagined view from nowhere.

Yogic and meditative traditions already cultivate attention to the formation of experience, the movement of thought, and the difference between a conditioned self-image and the knowing through which it appears, and our Śaiva sources treat these as matters of disciplined recognition, in which changing how one attends changes what becomes available to inquiry. Their scientific reception shows a consequential asymmetry, for a meditator is welcomed as someone able to produce an unusual brain state while their understanding of the experience is treated as a cultural interpretation to be set aside before the scientific account begins, so the practice supplies material and is denied the power to change the question. Neurophenomenology proposes a different relation, in which first-person descriptions and physiological findings constrain and refine one another, and its researchers identify the passage from experience back into the design and interpretation of experiments as the direction usually neglected.[^s01-neurophenomenology] Taking these sciences of consciousness seriously means letting their trained distinctions help decide what an adequate account must explain. Psychedelic inquiry makes the investigator's exposure especially palpable: in *LSD and the Mind of the Universe* the philosopher of religion Christopher M. Bache documents a twenty-year first-person experiment of seventy-three sessions and the long work of integrating them into ordinary life, through suffering, the loss of personal boundaries and an increasingly encompassing sense of participation in life.[^s01-bache] One can revise a scientific model and leave one's familiar identity undisturbed; in this kind of inquiry the habits by which one recognises oneself are exposed to transformation, and, as in the dream in which Jung killed Siegfried, his own heroic ideal, what undergoes revision includes the person judging the experience. Resistance may therefore protect something more intimate than a theoretical position. An investigator can be willing to question almost anything while refusing an inquiry in which their own settled sense of being someone becomes questionable. The arbiter thus feigns a stance of being beyond judgement or implication in the consequence of their methods, the pallid perfection of a view from nowhere; and yes, the unlocated Subject *is* viewing, and it *is* nowhere as such, and insofar as this is true it genuinely hasn't "a take" on the phenomena it takes ahold of. Mind is the one "on the take", the beneficiary and victim of its own *con*.

The history of discovery makes this dependence on experience hard to dismiss as a philosopher's concern. In his retrospective address of 1890 August Kekulé described approaching the structure of benzene through a dozing vision of snake-like forms, one of which seized its own tail and whirled before him; waking, he spent the rest of the night working out the hypothesis it suggested.[^s01-kekule] The serpent that swallows its tail, the ouroboros, is among the oldest images of a form returning into itself, and here it made a closed molecular arrangement thinkable: a relation became available to intuition before its chemical implications had been articulated, and that availability gave the reasoning its direction. Tesla described a deliberate cultivation of visual imagination in which he constructed, altered and ran machines before building them, detecting their faults within the imagined construction,[^s01-tesla] and Einstein, answering Jacques Hadamard's inquiry into mathematical thought, described visual and muscular elements he could combine before finding the words or signs to communicate their relations.[^s01-hadamard] In each case an investigator reached a relation through an image, a felt configuration or an internally enacted movement, and then gave others reasons by which the result became independently examinable; teaching those reasons need not reproduce the experience in which they were first found. Newton belongs in this history too: having studied his manuscripts, John Maynard Keynes described an intelligence whose sustained intuition preceded the proofs through which its conclusions became public, warned that the geometry of the *Principia* should not be mistaken for the process of its discovery, and found a Newton who inhabited a universe of divine signs, alchemical correspondences and a primordial revelation recoverable through nature and ancient texts.[^s01-keynes] The mathematician and the interpreter of mysteries were one person, and Keynes's name for him, "the last of the magicians", challenges the later portrait that keeps the mathematics and treats the rest as an embarrassing residue.

Other encounters changed what the investigator understood inquiry to concern. Emanuel Swedenborg's scientific work was followed by dreams, visions and the conversations with angels he described as opening a sustained engagement with the spiritual world, and in his own account his earlier study of nature had prepared him for that vocation.[^s01-swedenborg] Much later Federico Faggin, who led the design of the first commercial microprocessor, described an experience at Lake Tahoe in December 1990 in which he knew himself and the world as one self-knowing reality, with an overwhelming quality of love; the distinction between observer and observed changed within the experience, and its consequence was a lasting inquiry into consciousness.[^s01-faggin] An image can disclose a structure; an experience can change what counts as a question; a transformation in the investigator can alter what an adequate explanation must include. Rational demonstration belongs within this life of discovery, alongside the intuition that gives it something to demonstrate and the experience through which its adequacy is judged. A culture misrepresents its intelligence when it inherits the articulated result and expels the event that enabled the articulation, and its blind spot then conceals more than the first-person condition of evidence: it conceals the powers through which new evidence, new forms and new questions first become available.

**The Mind of Man is just a series of failed attempts to deal with the Unknown, in the process of which, however, we deal with a lot of unknowns.**[^s01-unknown] The failures are real, and so are the unknowns dealt with on the way: what we learn relieves suffering, exposes deceptions, corrects mistaken judgments and makes previously impossible acts available. Trouble begins with the further demand that an achievement abolish the relation on which it depends. The Greeks called that demand *hybris*, the overreach of a mortal into an authority not its own, and in our terms it is a finite measure appropriating the authority of the unmeasurable while the institution administering it treats its own perspective as the whole.[^s01-hybris] The paradoxes we have followed expose the cost of that demand from within the practices that make it, and their force is lost when they are taught as exceptional puzzles in the history of ideas while the inherited expectation of totality goes untouched. Epistemic humility is therefore a condition of beginning an honest inquiry, and it cannot be postponed until every confident attempt has failed. I already rely on ways of perceiving, distinguishing and recognising evidence whose justification I cannot complete before I use them, and to say "I must carry conditions and truths I cannot prove from within this account" declares that dependence at the outset, where it can still shape the design of the investigation. The opening of the torus belongs in the first drawing; it is not damage discovered after the surface has been completed. In practice this means stating what a method admits, what it brackets, and how an encounter could require those decisions to change, and the obligation holds alike for a laboratory, a political office and an artificial agent. A system that corrects its outputs while protecting every governing assumption has acquired only a limited competence in correction; humility requires the commission, the criterion and the claimed source of authority to remain examinable too.

These blind spots become political because a civilisation gives its ontology practical force through the institutions and technologies it builds. A person enters an administrative system under categories; a landscape becomes a field of resources; work becomes a quantity to be optimised; attention becomes something to predict and solicit. Each description permits real operations, and the operations alter the lives subsequently described, so everything turns on whether the people affected can change the terms under which the descriptions govern them. When only what can be rendered in the authorised account is allowed to revise that account, its administrators reproduce the closure we traced in the complex, their preferred picture supplying the criterion by which every challenge to the picture is judged, and the will to secure an advantage can pass for the defence of rational order until preserving one's position looks indistinguishable from preserving the truth. Whatever another person cannot make legible under that criterion is cancelled, `(−1)+(+1)=0`, as private sentiment, irrational resistance or irrelevant belief, while the advantage it protected is appropriated, `(+1)−(−1)=+2`, the same span counted against the dismissed as `(−1)−(+1)=−2`; an unfamiliar way of experiencing the world becomes a curiosity to classify, or an alien presence to fear, before it can become a reason to reconsider the classification. The denial of the Subject turns political at the moment it deprives a life of standing to question the means by which that life is administered, and what a culture lets people discover about themselves is bound up with how it expects them to live.

Across initiatory traditions the passage out of a familiar identity takes a communal form: separation from established surroundings, an interval in which ordinary assurances are suspended, and incorporation into new relationships and responsibilities. Ecstatic and psychedelic practices belong to some of these histories, their preparation and social reception helping decide what the experience becomes. Plato's divine mania returns here in its initiatory office: in the *Phaedrus* Socrates sets the prophetic inspiration of Delphi beside initiatory purification, poetry and the madness of love, and his own speech has to change, as what he first condemned as madness becomes something through which a person may receive more than their established understanding could provide.[^s01-phaedrus] Technical accomplishment alone cannot supply the inspiration its expression serves, and adept grammatical construction doesn't equate to a transmission of wisdom. Afterwards the experience can become a story, a teaching or a ritual through which others are prepared to meet something similar, one reason why, I surmise, scripture generally leverages so powerful a form as parable and poem; this stands out in stark relief in Kurt Flasch's naming of Meister Eckhart "the philosopher of Christianity".[^s01-flasch] Yet those expressions acquire a different authority when *amnesia* of the experiential origin degrades into memorising a teaching or conceptual form, and replaces actually *undergoing* and *wrestling with* what it concerns. A culture can preserve the words and images while losing the means of recognising what made them necessary, and its members inherit the resulting conventions through language, custom and the ordinary intimacy of being-with others, often long before they can tell what they have experienced from what they have learned to say. The initiatory question is whether that inheritance can expose a person to its own source, including an encounter that changes how the inheritance is understood. It doesn't help, of course, that traditional means of inducing such experiences are held under legal prohibition and treated with suspicion; superstition brandishes a rational judgement, from fear of an experience naturally overcoming of a mind's rigidity, as if hardwired networks of firing neuronal activity sought self-defence in the face of latent, potentially catalysed neuroplasticity.

Such an encounter challenges the authority of a person's account from a position the account did not authorise. Someone may have treated the boundaries of their familiar identity as the boundaries of experience itself, and what they undergo makes that identification impossible to maintain honestly; whether a more-than-human authority is at work becomes a question within the experience, instead of a proposition whose admissibility was decided beforehand. Asked in his 1959 television interview whether he believed in God, Jung answered, "I don't need to believe. I know." He gave the distinction its logic elsewhere: "Either I know a thing and then I don't need to believe it; or I believe it because I am not sure that I know it. I am well satisfied with the fact that I know experiences which I cannot avoid calling numinous or divine."[^s01-jung-know] He described a power unknown in itself that interrupted his intentions and changed the course of his life, located it in conscience, where he could argue with what exceeded his conscious will, and insisted that identifying his particular God-image with the whole of metaphysical reality would falsify what he knew; of the original behind our images, he wrote, he had no doubt, and it is inaccessible. In our terms his knowing was knowledge of an encounter, and his faith the continuation through what that knowing could not determine, the absolute limit lived as the basis of faith. This is the relation our definition asks us to keep: the experience gains authority to question the identity through which it was received, while the interpretation remains something the person must answer for, and indeed in some essential sense cannot doubt beyond. A judgment that excludes the encounter in advance cancels it, `(−1)+(+1)=0`, and protects the old account from examination; an interpretation that makes the encounter a warrant for its own infallibility appropriates its authority anew, `(+1)−(−1)=+2`, and every doubt is entered on the other side as `(−1)−(+1)=−2`. **Deferential Intelligence** begins where what has been encountered can change the means by which the next judgment is made, in a sense that echoes the traditional and often fraught notion of a *covenant*, or at the very least an apparently bidirectional and intentional relation (in the overdetermined senses of intent and "aboutness", philosophically speaking).

Belonging changes too: observational research associates shared psychedelic experiences with increased social connectedness, and prospective research links changes in self-experience with a stronger felt relationship to nature.[^s01-connectedness] But even having to present this as "evidence" buries the point; what connection to tradition has been severed, and what effect has this on the knower, its means of knowing, and the world it knows? Either way, these findings turn attention toward what people afterwards recognise as part of the life to which they belong, often reported to be the larger, more meaningful part. Jung's concept of individuation describes a differentiation of the person that deepens their collective relationships: he opposed any levelling of a life down to collective standards, which for him *averaged* out the uniqueness of the individual, while recognising that the individual's existence already presupposes relations with others, so that individuation must lead "to more intense and broader collective relationships and not to isolation".[^s01-jung-individuation] Insofar as that relation involves tension, influence and interdependence, this uniqueness remains a hard-won gem, such is the allure to fledgling ego of fitting in. Jung's sense of the word as becoming an *in-dividual*, an indivisible unity or whole, turns this into a question of wholeness: can a person acknowledge more of the relations through which they are themselves, including those their preferred identity had excluded? Becoming more fully this particular life can mean becoming less defensively separate because, paradoxically, it seems *the more I know my own centre as everywhere, the more I can live with a circumference nowhere*. Passing from childhood to adulthood then involves more than replacing the parent's commands with one's own. It includes discovering that the authority to act carries obligations neither the parent nor the individual simply invented, and that the question of one's own existential condition is a question native to inter-subjective living. Someone who experiences their dependence on a living earth, or some notion of transcendent dimensionality, acquires another reason to question what may be demanded in the name of private advantage or public claim. Initiatory transformation becomes consequential through such changes in attention, judgment and responsibility, but above all in the change, or perhaps utter transvaluation, of one's values.

These capacities have a sociopolitical history as well as a psychological one, and the historical importance of the drug trade for various empires supplies a geopolitical-economic one too. Hannah Arendt distinguished the solitude in which someone can *think with themselves* from the loneliness in which they lose a dependable relation both to others and to their own experience: isolation diminishes the capacity to act together, and loneliness makes the promise of total belonging especially powerful.[^s01-arendt] A person needs others through whom their experience can be tested and their identity recognised, and also enough inward freedom to question what the group tells them; destroying those relations leaves the demand for assurance intact and hands it to an authority promising the whole answer. Gustave Le Bon, writing from very different political commitments, examined how prestige lends a person, title or idea the force to command assent before its claims are examined.[^s01-le-bon] Repetition and suggestion, the method and magic of media and propaganda, let an inherited judgment feel like one's own, while distance preserves the authority it came from. Our family metaphor already showed the possibility both describe: someone can become highly competent while continuing to seek from an institution the assurance once sought from a parent, and the totalitarian societies Arendt studied revealed how far an unintelligent deference tends toward banal but definite atrocity. The capacity to perform a role can develop much further than the capacity to question why that role has become the measure of one's worth, and indeed under the command of authority, the freedom to act from one's own moral intention can become warped to the point of utter inversion.

Where adulthood is measured chiefly by earning, acquiring and remaining compliant enough to be employable and reliable enough to carry out one's given-cum-imposed responsibilities, this disparity serves an extractive economy. Our institutions reward competence while keeping recognition conditional on further performance, so that a person works to obtain what they need and also to secure permission to regard themselves as sufficient, perhaps worthy in some clear or arcane sense. Material dependence reinforces the arrangement whenever housing, food and the care of others hang on continued approval, a *sword of Damocles* hanging above. A society organised this way can encourage arrested development by cultivating the particular dependence it profits from: people learn to administer their own exhaustion, discipline their objections and interpret their difficulty in meeting the demand as a private failure. Hannah Arendt saw this going as far as friends and family policing, and ultimately consigning to death, their loved ones in the name of appeasing the figure or force that, for them, authors and sets order. Heidegger's concept of **Bestand**, standing-reserve, names beings, forces and nature as such becoming ordered, *digitised*, and packaged and stored as resource held ready for future use, effectively removed from any prior implicit participation in processes concerning anything beyond the desire for that resource.[^s01-bestand] The same dynamic, I'd say, acquires an inward counterpart when people learn to experience their own worth chiefly through their availability. A facet of our political argument is that the meaning crisis is sustained partly through this substitution of functional recruitment for supported individuation: a person can be initiated thoroughly into the demands of an organisation while remaining unable to recognise how that organisation has acquired authority over their sense of being someone, and I sense in this a concomitant *crisis of purpose*. An encounter that makes the life answerable to more than its allotted role interrupts that authority; indeed being less answerable, beholden, to the demands of "this world" appears a supreme freedom, and pragmatically we catch sight of its effect, real or performed, in the charisma and magnetism of those we grant power, *influential remit*, to. Recognising one's belonging to other beings, a living earth and a dynamic cosmos supplies grounds for refusing demands that an employer, market or state presents as unavoidable, and such recognition threatens an extractive arrangement because it changes what the person is willing to sacrifice for its approval. More palpably, such experiences of expansive identification tend to simply dwarf the value of those making demands from a more myopic and narcissistic basis.

The meaning crisis therefore concerns how a common world-sense is organised, and a shortage of comforting stories to accompany its operation is the least of it. A civilisation cannot keep treating first-person significance as secondary to what can be extracted, measured and managed, and then expect its members to find meaning secure, or motivation and *joie de vivre* readily available. People learn to recognise themselves through the categories that make them useful or visible and to distrust what they cannot express in those terms. Institutions can even acknowledge the reality of inner transformation while accepting only the consequences that restore a person's usefulness: the experience is welcome if its effects become useful to *an outside party*. These assumptions travel through infrastructures, classifications and standards, deciding what can circulate, what receives support and which forms of knowledge may influence a decision, and their reach can become global while the worldview they come from remains partial. A genuinely global culture would have to permit more than the inclusion of other traditions as material within its own account: those traditions, and the experiences through which they understand themselves, must be able to question how the common account is made. The question of an integral epistemology is already a question of power, whether different ways of knowing can meet while keeping the capacity to alter the conditions of their meeting.

Physics itself gives reasons to doubt the picture of independent things whose relations are added afterward. In a quantum superposition the amplitudes associated with alternatives contribute to interference, and replacing their coherent relation with a classical either-or distribution changes the predictions; the distinction has observable consequences. Technical command of this relation leaves untouched how a person understands identity, authority or disagreement, so we can calculate through a non-classical formalism and still demand that a social or metaphysical question resolve into two independently complete opposing terms. Digital technology shows the same disparity, since its zeroes and ones are both determinate states, both available for discrimination and manipulation, and both belong to the `1`-side of our symbolon; a culture can multiply their computational power while leaving the `0/`, the unobjectifiable condition and the mediating relation, out of its understanding of itself. A civilisation built with zeroes has not thereby learned what its zero excludes. `0/1` asks for superposition to become intelligible as a relation we can consciously sustain, including where its opposed readings concern our own identities.

What we have gathered here leaves a cultural question: how can a civilisation acknowledge the limits of its knowledge while continuing to organise life, however tacitly, around the promise of a final account? People inherit more than propositions. We learn what deserves attention, whose judgment carries authority and which aspirations make a life worthwhile, and questioning those habits unsettles the world in which we learned to recognise ourselves; yet the capacities developed within that world also produce circumstances its accustomed thinking can no longer address. Decadence is an achievement defended beyond the reach of its usefulness, its powers preserved while its purposes grow harder to justify. Surviving a bust by preparing the next boom may restore the economy while leaving the household, the *oikos* it is supposed to sustain, less able to care for its members. Renewal would require learning from what the crisis exposes, including where the inherited measure of success helped produce it, and telling the recovery of an established order apart from a mutation in how a world is understood. Jean Gebser made that distinction his life's subject, following how consciousness becomes manifest in language, art, science and the organisation of everyday experience, and the integral advent he described is the mutation: people recognise the conditions of their own seeing and gain through that recognition a different capacity to inhabit the world they are helping to make.

---

<a id="M05"></a>
<!-- movement:M05 -->

To paint a room in perspective, an artist must first decide where it is being seen from, and every other decision follows from that one. The far wall shrinks against the near one in exact proportion to its distance; the floor's edges converge toward a single point on the horizon; a figure's height is fixed by how far it stands from the chosen eye; colour cools and pales into the distance to carry the illusion further. Every line in the picture runs toward a point that holds nothing, the vanishing point, an empty place that nonetheless organises the whole count of the scene. These relations let the painting disclose a coherent space, and they also place its viewer: the picture is already addressed to someone standing, imaginatively, *here*, at the one spot from which all its lines resolve. Jean Gebser read the emergence of perspective as a mutation in how people stood toward their world. In *The Ever-Present Origin* he followed it through Giotto's first weighted bodies and stage-like depth, Petrarch's ascent of Mont Ventoux and his new apprehension of landscape, Alberti's construction of the visual pyramid, and Leonardo's work across painting, optics and technical invention.[^s01-gebser] Through them the relation between artistic intuition and mathematical construction turned from symbolic placement into measured projection. On the medieval painted surface the size and placement of figures expressed their rank within a sacred story; perspective made their appearance answerable to a further relation, the distance between an object and an eye, and a flat surface became the means of constructing a coherent three-dimensional space. In essence, Mind acquired a method for plotting positions from positions: by laying out a grid, it could gather objects into a common spatial order and calculate relations that no isolated object supplied. Space became something one could work through as a matrix, so that a building could be planned, an instrument aimed and an arrangement tested before anything was built. Yet a finished picture could also conceal the position that made its precision possible, and present a view from somewhere as simply how things are. Gebser's inquiry meets our reading of Descartes at exactly this point, where doubt found the doubter already present in its own performance. Whoever looks remains involved in producing the view, while the view appears to contain everything relevant to what is seen, and our definition of the Subject explains why another picture of the observer cannot close that enclosure.

Working from inside Western metaphysics, Martin Heidegger reached the same event in "The Age of the World Picture", where he argues that the modern age is defined by a change in how beings as a whole become present: the world is no longer pictured, it is conceived and grasped *as* picture, and that event is one and the same as the human being becoming *subiectum*, the relational centre on which every being is made to depend.[^s01-heidegger] What Gebser reads in Giotto's depth and Alberti's pyramid as the perspectival fixing of a sector, Heidegger reads as *Vorstellen*, representing: the setting of the world before a subject who measures it. The two arrived at their diagnoses independently, and we pair them because each supplies what the other withholds: Gebser the art-historical body and an integral future, Heidegger the metaphysical mechanism and its technological consequence, the world ordered as *Bestand*, standing-reserve, the culture that learns to measure people by their availability. The eye at the apex of Alberti's pyramid is the knower made into a place, and our notation writes that place as `Ø`, the crossed zero: the zero of the Subject crossed by the stroke of its own mediation, so that the slash through which a world is seen fuses with the one who sees and the picture's means pass for its source. §1 introduces the sign and its history, and §4 follows the sequence by which the crossing comes undone. Its solution begins wherever the stroke is recognised as the mediation it is, and the zero behind it is found empty in the most generous sense: a place that holds nothing and, counted, becomes the first one from which every further count proceeds.

In *Prometheus and Atlas* Jason Reza Jorjani carries the same event into myth, reading technoscience through two titans.[^s01-jorjani] Prometheus is its exploratory and challenging drive: forethought, the theft of fire, the defiant provocation of whatever is given. Atlas is its world-bearing and world-picturing power, the titan who holds a world up and maps it, the condition of there being any world-picture at all. The perspectival picture is Atlas's burden, a whole world held up from one standing place, crushing whoever takes the weight for the only way a world can be carried. Prometheus is the fire that made the picture possible, the forethinking light of reason taken for human use, and Jung reads the Promethean figure as a figure of the Logos.[^s01-jung-prometheus] In the myth Prometheus pays for the fire chained to the rock, an eagle tearing at his liver each day, and Atlas can never set the sky down. A culture that has taken the fire and holds its world up as a picture inherits both punishments, an exposure that reopens daily and a burden it cannot rest. An integral response cannot hand the fire back, and finding a stronger titan to take the weight would only renew the sentence. Its best hope is that thought's fire consume the very notion of *the world*, the concept in which we have housed the separations and conflagrations of our thinking. Every culture has carried an image of the world's end, by flood, fire or judgment, and that image is live again in our fears of collapse; read notionally, the end it foretells is the end of a category, the world as one picture held up from one place. What survives the burning is worldhood, the relation in which anything becomes a world for someone, which no single picture has to contain. Atlas can then set the sky down, because nobody has to carry a whole that was never a thing, and §3's answer keeps his name: an atlas of charts, in which every local map keeps its frame visible and no single picture has to bear the whole.

Gebser came to this question as a poet and translator as well as a philosopher, through a Europe whose technical and intellectual accomplishments had failed to prevent its devastation. He left Germany in 1929, lived in Spain and then France, and reached Switzerland shortly before its borders closed in 1939, and his work among writers and artists grew through these repeated displacements. His life put to a whole civilisation the question that Russell, Gödel and Wittgenstein met at the limits of formal reason: how could people become so capable of organising their world and remain so unable to understand the forces through which they organised it? Precision in carrying out an intention gives no assurance that the intention has been examined, and a society can train its members to justify what they do while leaving untouched the image of themselves that demands the justification. Gebser looked to art because mutations surface there first, and Jung explained why they do: art, he wrote, is "constantly at work educating the spirit of the age", since the artist's unsatisfied yearning reaches back to the primordial image best fitted to compensate the one-sidedness of the present and shapes that image into a form contemporaries can receive.[^s01-jung-art] In typological terms artists lead with intuition and feeling more than with thinking. They embody and manifest a mutation before anyone formalises it as a concept, so that a new way of painting is a new way of learning to see: an intuition becomes a public object, and others develop through the encounter a capacity its maker first exercised without being able to explain it.

Sri Aurobindo pursued the transformation of consciousness from within a different political struggle. Educated in England, he became a writer and organiser for Indian independence, advocating *swaraj* and challenging British authority through non-cooperation and the creation of Indian institutions. Arrested in the Alipore conspiracy case in 1908, he spent a year in detention before his acquittal, and his practice of yoga deepened in prison. He moved to Pondicherry in 1910 and there developed the philosophical and spiritual work published as *The Life Divine* and *The Synthesis of Yoga*, aiming at a transformation of mind, life and body through consciousness becoming aware of its divine source. **Supermind** named a knowledge in which the One's differentiation into the Many proceeds through self-knowledge, so that unity never has to be reconstructed from things first assumed to exist apart. In his 1966 preface Gebser set Aurobindo's supramental beside the integral consciousness whose manifestations he had been tracing, and Ulrich Mohrhoff later developed the comparison.[^s01-aurobindo-gebser] Both men asked what becomes possible when the reality through which a person knows is itself recognised in the knowing.

In a letter later discussed by Georg Feuerstein, Gebser described the question coming home to him as experience, in an awakening at Sarnath in 1961: everyday perception continued alongside extraordinary clarity, quiet jubilation and **primordial trust**.[^s01-sarnath] His essay "Homologies in Spiritual Attitude" described a transparency grounded in non-duality, in which concrete things remained present while the opposition separating them from the one experiencing them no longer governed the encounter, and he insisted that this was an intensification of consciousness whose realisation carried a responsibility toward humanity. What deepened in him has two Śaiva names, **pratyabhijñā**, recognition, and **samāveśa**, entry or immersion, in which what was known as a proposition about consciousness becomes the reality through which a person experiences their world. His primordial trust is the trust of the child who can ask because a language and a relation already bear the asking, the dependence already enacted in `0/1`. Of the four offices we gave that dependence when the household's reliance came under question, trust, knowledge, belief and faith, Gebser met the first in its pure form at Sarnath, with no account standing between him and what sustained him: *fides*, the reliance already in force before any arbitration, from which *credere*, the renewed entrustment made once an undertaking has returned its consequences, can later be given.[^s01-credere] Our language of knowing is economic from the start, since we *credit* a report, *claim* a truth, give an *account* and assign *value*. Every belief and every thought draws on a credit it did not create, the reliability of language, memory, testimony and the senses, and then takes credit for the truth it borrows, presenting as its own achievement a reliance that was extended to it. Descartes met this primacy of trust when his doubt reached its inherited assurances and found itself drawing on them. Where the borrowed credit goes unacknowledged, knowledge takes the fork we watched the parent and the governing office take, turning from a support under uncertainty into a register of whom to distrust. Primordial trust reverses the direction of the account. A person recognises that the reality they had asked an explanation to guarantee was already sustaining the asking; dependence becomes directly knowable as belonging, the account is relieved of the impossible demand that it first create the ground it stands on, and credit can be extended again knowingly, as *credere*.

Gebser called the major shifts in how this belonging is lived **mutations of consciousness**, and he distinguished five structures: **archaic, magical, mythical, mental and integral**. They are easiest to feel through an image every reader carries, the span of a single life. An infant lives before any division of self and world. A small child lives where names and things, wishes and events, still touch. An older child lives in stories, in a drama of powers and persons. An adolescent learns to argue, to ask for reasons and to stand apart. A mature adult, at best, holds all of these at once and knows which is speaking, and an elder, at the best of old age, knows them as one life. None of the earlier layers is lost: they settle beneath the later ones, and an adult who curses a stubborn machine, falls into a story or is moved by a ritual is living from them still. Gebser's structures are this layering at the scale of a civilisation, a transpersonal and collective history, and Erich Neumann argued in the same spirit that the growing ego recapitulates a history of consciousness that myth already carries.[^s01-neumann] The ages are long and their borders blurred: archaic participation before anything we would call history; magical life in the clan and the hunt; mythical consciousness in the oral epics and sacred kingdoms of the early civilisations; the mental structure from the Greek invention of argued philosophy to its perspectival consummation in the Renaissance; and the integral, whose signs Gebser read in the art, physics and thought of the twentieth century. Each structure extends credit to a different authority, the magical to sympathetic bonds, the mythical to the story and its gods, the mental to argued reason, and each is tempted to take credit for a truth the others helped it find. Jung's psychic functions give a third reading of the same sequence, as the Logos developing through one function after another.[^s01-functions-mutations] Sensation leads in the magical structure, feeling in the mythical, thinking in the mental, and intuition in the integral, where intuition holds thinking and feeling together as one capacity. Gebser's **Origin** is the spiritual ground of them all, and Jung had already found words for it in *The Red Book*: "the primordial beginning, which at the same time is what has been and what is becoming".[^s01-redbook-origin] The beginning is released from the merely past, originary because what issues from it goes on carrying its becoming, and Gebser's *Ever-Present Origin* gives that temporal paradox its cultural architecture: Origin is the source through which history takes its successive forms, never the first item on a historical line. Sought only behind us, it becomes a lost object of history; identified with the present arrangement, it leaves that arrangement no room to change; deferred to the future, it stays absent instead of being the power through which becoming happens now. Like the child, it refers two ways at once. Jung saw the child archetype as both beginning and end, "an initial and a terminal creature": it "existed before man was" and "will be when man is not."[^s01-jung-child] The first-person mystery of being born and a birthless metaphysical Origin speak through one image. In our terms Origin is the unobjectifiable Subject whose differentiation we have written as `0/1`, the One that "Subject" and "God" both sign, the uncarved block that stays one through every carving.

Archaic consciousness is an undifferentiated participation in that Origin, before self, world, soul and nature are experienced as separate terms that an explanation must later bridge. Gebser called it zero-dimensional because no position yet exists from which an independent "I" surveys an external world, and the unity has not yet been recognised through the differences it contains. Archaic participation and integral recognition concern the same Origin under profoundly different conditions: what the infant lives without knowing, the mature adult has to realise through everything that has since become distinct.

In the **magical** structure a centre begins to emerge and to exert a will on what surrounds it, and it acts through **sympathy**. A name, a figure, a lock of hair or a carved image stands for its referent by sympathetic identity. It does more than represent the thing; it shares in it, so that what is done to the image is done, in some measure, to what it images. Such a bond resembles an entanglement, two things that stay joined across a distance, and its effects are psychological where they are not literal, as anyone knows who has flinched at a photograph torn in anger. Jung read the energy of this world through *mana*, the Melanesian word, as Codrington reported it, for an extraordinary power that is fixed in nothing and can be conveyed into almost anything: a stone, a chief, a word, a place. For Jung *mana* is the primitive form of the concept of psychic energy, the potency running through whatever affects a life.[^s01-mana] In Gebser's account ritual, incantation and trance express this participation, and he counted telepathy and clairvoyance among the capacities of a consciousness not yet governed by ordinary spatial and temporal separation. Sensation is the leading function, since the magical world is met in its raw, immediate force before any story is told about it. Magic is also the first home of the matheme, since a mark or a number that acts on what it marks is magic's own exact sign, and the formal inscriptions of later mathematics keep that efficacy in a disciplined shape. Our *symbolon* explains why a mark can act: the part approaches the whole through its belonging, and the act takes its force from that belonging. Will brings a struggle over power with it, because what sustains a life can also threaten it, and a person seeks ways to ward off, enlist or command those powers; the desire to control is present long before the rational techniques that later give it other means.

In the **mythical** structure those powers become distinguishable through their relations and transformations, and the means of knowing is the told story. Oral history carries a people's experience across generations by compressing it: centuries of encounter with storm, harvest, death, desire and betrayal condense into a few figures and their deeds, so that a myth is psychodynamics compressed into a form that can be remembered and retold. Raw force, which magical consciousness met through sensation, becomes a god with a name, a temper and a history, and the encounter with it becomes a relation one can feel one's way into. Feeling leads here, since the mythical world is known through what things are worth, whom they bind and whom they wound. Day and night, life and death, mother and father are polarities within a shared reality, each receiving its character through the other, and seasonal rites give time a rhythm in which loss and renewal are understood together. Myth is the first home of the mytheme, the image that proves a relation through the action it stages. Modern habit treats myth as an invented explanation laid over a self-evident material world, and so reverses the situation. For mythical consciousness the gods are powers through which the world is met at all; they give desire, conflict and transformation an experienced form long before anyone classifies them as psychological contents. A telling turns restrictive when every new encounter must repeat an inherited role, and that restriction depends on myth's real capacity to make a life intelligible.

In the **mental** structure those inherited forms become available to explicit examination, and the story becomes an *episteme*, a reasoned science of the world and of Mind. A speaker distinguishes a claim from its bearer, asks for reasons, follows consequences and compares an explanation with what it purports to explain; a god can become the subject of a proposition whose truth is questioned, and the proposition no longer borrows authority from the divine name merely because the name occurs in it. With thinking leading, a person gains a definite position from which to judge, and the world is set before that position: painting's three-dimensional, perspectival achievement made into a way of knowing. Objects can be located in a common space, their relations calculated and their behaviour tested by procedures others can repeat, and this makes possible the sustained criticism of appearances that ran from Russell and Whitehead to Gödel. It also tempts mental consciousness to take reality for whatever its own criticism can admit. Images are then treated as ornaments, magic as error and spiritual recognition as a belief awaiting a better explanation. Mental consciousness turns deficient when its power to distinguish hardens into the authority to disqualify every other way of knowing, including those through which its own purposes and discoveries arise: it takes credit for a truth it holds in common. The previous movement has already shown that deficient phase arising from rationality's own products. Russell's paradox, Gödel's sentence and the hard problem were each reached by reasoning at its most exact, and each brought reason to a threshold it had produced for itself, a point where its own procedures returned what they could not render rational. The deficiency shows in how that threshold is met. Gebser insists on the difference between the *irrational* and the **arational**: the irrational falls below measure and disregards it, while the arational knows and respects the measurable and is free of it, and it is impossible without the pre-rational, the irrational and the rational, which together form its basis.[^s01-gebser-arational] Deficient mental consciousness can meet its threshold only as the irrational, something to exclude, fear or explain away, which is why Russell sought certainty against paradox and why a culture defers its blind spot to a future physics. Met as the arational, the same threshold is where the integral begins: the contradiction becomes, in Whitehead's words, "the first step in progress towards a victory", and the limit becomes the basis of faith as well as of investigation.

Wolfgang Giegerich's *The End of Meaning and the Birth of Man* describes the severity of this historical shift as a birth out of the encompassing world of inherited meaning.[^s01-giegerich] What once contained the person becomes something the person can set before themselves and evaluate. A myth deliberately chosen to restore that containment already bears the mark of the freedom from it that made choosing possible, so modern consciousness cannot simply decide to recover the innocence of being held by an unquestioned world. Freedom from the old forms leaves our relation to the powers they expressed unsettled. Many gods can be subordinated to one God, their conflicts interpreted as events within the psyche, and divine sovereignty finally rejected in favour of a self-sufficient material universe, while desire, terror, devotion and the need to belong go on organising life through every shift. A society may cease to recognise the sacred character of what it serves while investing nation, market or technical progress with demands once made in the name of God: its account turns secular while its submission remains absolute. We face a post-symbolic exposure. We are responsible for understanding powers that the inherited images no longer authoritatively explain, and that rational description alone has not brought under conscious command.

The **integral** structure requires these ways of knowing to become present together in their actual operation, and Gebser called that co-presence **diaphaneity**, a showing-through. Things keep their particularity while their appearance discloses the relations through which we meet them: a mathematical description is understood together with its assumptions, the intuition that prompted it and the purposes directing its use; a sacred image is recognised both in what it reveals and in how it organises a person's hopes and fears. The integral stands at **#4**, the position QL names Context, and it works by contextualising. Magic's efficacious sign, myth's proving image and the mental's warranted account, the first homes of matheme, mytheme and episteme, each claimed to be the whole of knowing because each forgot the circumstances in which it knows. Diaphaneity lets those circumstances show through, so that each way of knowing appears within the context of its use, and that sight voids its claim to stand outside every context. Their difference stays and their rivalry for sole possession of truth ends. The voiding is the first act in recognising the symbolon, since pieces that had each mistaken themselves for the whole are found to fit. Intuition leads in this structure because it can hold the fit: it is the unity of thinking and feeling, the capacity to grasp a whole situation with judgment and with care at once.

What the integral recognises in Logos is a recovery of what the word always carried. *Logos* comes from *legein*, whose earlier senses are to gather, to pick up and lay together, and then to count and recount, before it settles into *to speak*; Heidegger read Heraclitus' Logos as this laying that gathers.[^s01-legein] Jung's pairing of Logos as discrimination, judgment and insight with Eros as the placing into relation describes two motions the root already held together, and Russell's desire for certainty was that eros already at work in a logician. A Logos that only cuts has forgotten its own gathering, and the integral remembers it, so the symbolon it recognises is the symbolon of the eros of Logos. Gebser's *aperspectival* follows from this recovery: perspective loses its exclusive authority, and we can occupy a position clearly while recognising the other structures through which it has become meaningful. His *arational* names a reasoning that no longer requires reality to end at reason's present boundary.

Aurobindo's **Supermind** names the affirmative shift that follows the voiding at **#4**: where the integral sees each structure in its context, in the supramental a person identifies as the totality of the mutations, the archaic participant, the magician, the teller of myths and the reasoner known as the layers of one life and owned without any one of them presiding, as an elder at best owns every age they have lived. That identification never settles into a possession: it is a constant passage between **#4** and **#5**, Context and Quintessence, in which every new context is received into the whole and the whole is tested again in every context. `5→0` names the further turn, the spiritually realised state in which one who identifies as the totality of the mutations identifies also with and as the Origin, what always is and what is becoming through all of them. Our opening question, "What knows?", now has a clearer answer: Origin knows, through every structure in which it becomes manifest, and the person asking knows as Origin as far as that identification reaches; a world gathered into an account includes the gathering through which it becomes this account.

The structures do not follow one another like slides in a projector, and two thinkers name the two dimensions of the **thickness of phenomenal experience** that explain why. Henri Bergson began *Time and Free Will* (1889) against the habit of measuring lived time as if it were space, in which the intellect sets moments side by side like points on a line and arrives at a homogeneous, countable time that nobody has ever lived. What we live he called **durée**, duration: the continuous qualitative life in which what has just occurred permeates what is occurring now, so that successive states melt into one another and form a whole without ever becoming a row.[^s01-bergson] Listening to a tune, we hear each sounding note through the phrase already underway, and the note can fulfil an expectation, prolong a tension or change how the earlier notes are heard; set out note by note, the same tune turns into a picture of a melody, the spatialised time Bergson diagnosed. He argued in the same book that a free act springs from the whole self living in duration, and he later made intuition the method by which philosophy enters duration from within, which brings his thought close to the function that leads the integral.[^s01-bergson-intuition] Turn within the painter's room and one wall passes out of view while another appears, yet we go on inhabiting one room; what has disappeared stays implicated in our orientation, and returning to the first view does not restore the first experience unchanged, because we have lived through the turn. Edmund Husserl named the other dimension **sedimentation**: what a culture once grasped with full insight settles into its language, habits and instruments, where later generations inherit it as obvious, usable and largely unexamined, until someone reactivates the act that first gave it sense.[^s01-husserl] Duration thickens experience across moments; sedimentation thickens it across generations. Gebser's mutations stack in exactly this way, as layers laid down in the collective mind and, within each life, in the personal mind, laminated so that a magical reflex, a mythic loyalty and a mental argument can all act in a single gesture. Bratton reaches for the same image when he describes how an artificial agent is composed: "not a monad but a lamination", in which the persona is one stratum among many.[^s01-bratton-lamination] Bergson's duration folds through these laminae: the past is active within present understanding and never deposited outside it as a finished object, so a later discovery can alter what an earlier event meant. In this layered thickness Objective Internality becomes palpable, as the relation in which objects become subjective forms and the Subject knows through them. Gebser's *time-freedom* is release from the exclusive rule of any one temporal form: magical participation, mythical rhythm and mental succession become consciously available within the present without any one being declared real at the expense of the others, and the ever-present Origin becomes recognisable through the whole differentiated life, the history of its recognition included.

Gebser's dimensional progression follows increasingly adequate ways of articulating a reality that includes the act of articulation. Perspective coordinates appearances from a position; integral knowing includes the lived transformations through which positions become available to one another, and §3 constructs these relations mathematically, through rotation, projection and double covering. When the QL positions are assigned, they will have to express one activity, Mind gathering a world through forms of knowing that are themselves present within that world. Each of those forms extends credit, issues claims, keeps accounts and assigns value, and the words for these operations belong to economy, whose name comes from the household.

Greek **oikonomia**, from which we get *economy*, meant the management of an **oikos**, a household: the people, provisions, property and work through which a shared life was maintained. Its *nom-* belongs to *nemein*, to distribute, allot or pasture, and **nomos**, custom and law, began as an allotted grazing before it became the ordering through which distributions are established; the *nomad* still moves between such allotments, and *anomie* names a life left without its share. A younger word for the same house is just as exact: Ernst Haeckel coined *Oekologie* in 1866 from *oikos* joined to *logos*, so that economy and ecology name the two gestures of one household: *nemein* distributes it, and *legein* gathers it into an account of what keeps it alive.[^s01-oikos] A mind distributes attention and gathers what it has attended to into a world; a life distributes energy among its organs and gathers them into one metabolism; a world distributes places among its beings and gathers them into a common habitation. An economy that has lost its *logos* goes on allotting a house it no longer accounts for, and the ecological crisis is that loss written into the earth. These words take us into a world where knowing how to live already involves deciding what belongs to whom, who may act, and what each owes to the others. Aristotle's household included relations between parent and child, husband and wife, master and enslaved person; its unity was organised through unequal powers, and the one who administered it did not necessarily recognise everyone sustaining it as equally entitled to determine its purposes. His distinction between household provision and unlimited acquisition, *chrēmatistikē*, still holds for the same reason: obtaining more is a different undertaking from providing what a life together requires, and an economy can become highly effective at the first while damaging the relations that make the second possible. He kept his sharpest judgment for usury, because Greek called interest *tokos*, offspring, and money bred from money seemed to him the most unnatural of births.[^s01-nomisma] A household is also a family, its generations overlapping, each inheriting a share it did not earn and owing a care it did not choose. Economics and law, number and word, seem to be the primary determinants of life as it is lived, personal and familial before they become national or global.

Value and rule meet explicitly in **nomisma**, coin or current money. In the *Nicomachean Ethics* Aristotle connected the word with *nomos*: money lets unlike goods and different kinds of work be compared through an accepted measure whose value depends on convention.[^s01-nomisma] A coin is itself a `0/1`: the blank disc is its ground, the die strikes a determination into it, and current value is their relation. Greek called the die-stamp *charaktēr* and the impression *typos*, from which an *archetype*, a first impression, still takes its name; around the face runs the legend, *legenda*, what is to be read, and the denomination gives the measure a name and a count. A stamped token circulates because people recognise what may be claimed through it, though the token itself neither produces the provisions nor performs the labour for which it is exchanged. *Token* grows from the root *\*deik-*, to show or point, which also gives *index* and *digit*, and a token points past itself to a trust already extended, so that its value is a presence held on loan. *Currency* runs, from *currere*, and what is current is whatever is present enough to pass from hand to hand; money borrows that presence from the work, goods and lives it points to, and *value*, from *valere*, to be strong or well, is a strength it keeps only while the loan is honoured. We approach an **economy of mind** through this relation between a sign, its accepted value and the order in which it becomes effective. A divine name, an ancestral title, a legal category or a numerical score directs attention and conduct because people have learned what it authorises. Through *legein*, gathering and saying, they bring experience into a communicable account; through their *nomos*, they establish which accounts may govern what happens next. Knowing and ruling are already distinct, and already entangled, within the use of language itself: calling someone a child, a citizen, a subject or a resource assigns them a place from which quite different claims may be heard. English keeps that entanglement in its oldest words of exchange, since *owe* and *own* grow from one root, *āgan*, to have, a *debt* is *de-habere*, something had from another, and *interest*, *inter-esse*, is what lies between. Interest charged on a loan prices the between itself, the very slash through which two parties are related, and counts it to one side: the appropriation given a rate, the lender's `(+1)−(−1)=+2` being the borrower's `(−1)−(+1)=−2`.[^s01-economy-words]

An *oikonomia* therefore concerns how a whole sustains its differentiated members and what becomes of the powers apportioned among them, well beyond an inventory of possessions. Ephesians extends the word to the divine ordering of the fullness of times, *oikonomia tou plērōmatos*, in which what belongs to heaven and earth is gathered together in Christ, so that the administration of a household becomes a language for manifestation, history and their fulfilment.[^s01-ephesians] The Church Fathers went on to distinguish within the divine life itself between *theologia*, God as God is, and *oikonomia*, God's dispensation in creation and history (Tertullian's *dispensatio*, a weighing-out), and Western theology has laboured ever since to keep the two from coming apart, until Karl Rahner restated their unity in his rule that the economic Trinity is the immanent Trinity.[^s01-theologia-oikonomia] Read through our notation, the divine economy is syn-ballein, `(0/1)/(1/0)`: the One dispensed into the Many and the Many gathered back, with neither half becoming the owner of the whole. Kashmiri Śaivism holds the relation as a co-equal pair. Śiva names the Subject in its speakable aspect, and Śakti, his consort, is the *power* of that Subject to differentiate and take on identities both as means of knowing and as objects of knowledge: Śiva is Mono, Śakti is Poly. Such metaphysical dynamics cannot remain abstract when they account for the same dynamics that decide whether or not I get to eat tomorrow, or have a house to hold me at all. Every finite capacity operates within a reality it does not privately originate: whoever knows receives language, whoever commands receives an office, and whoever makes something receives materials and powers of making. Independence is a particular achievement within dependence. Sovereignty turns into a problem when an allotted capacity is used to claim possession of the whole it derives from, and the inversion lies already in the ground of our own terms. Our Poly is *polys*, many; the *poly* of *monopoly* is *pōlein*, to sell, so that in a monopoly the One stands where the many should be and faces, in their place, only what is sold. Aristotle records the first instance in the very chapter where he separates provision from acquisition: Thales, foreseeing a large olive harvest, hired every press in Miletus and Chios and let them out at whatever price he chose.[^s01-monopoly] The diabolical economy is dia-ballein, its own namesake. Its polar form, `(−1)/(+1)`, holds two parties in tension across a relation both depend on; its collapsed form, `(−1)±(+1)`, either cancels them into `0` or lets one of them count the whole span as its own, `+2` or `−2`. The `1` then takes its own measure as the measure of reality, and those who cannot live under it are required to explain their failure.

The Dutch **Vereenigde Oostindische Compagnie**, the VOC, gave this appropriation a formidable institutional form, one that in many ways became the blueprint of both colonial and post-colonial institutional power and runs through to the present day. Established in 1602, it received a trading monopoly together with powers to conclude agreements with foreign rulers, build forts, administer justice, employ soldiers and wage war.[^s01-voc] It was also among the first companies whose shares changed hands publicly, on the Amsterdam exchange, and English still names such a holding *stock*, after the creditor's half of the split tally stick whose halves were once rejoined to prove a debt: a *symbolon* become a holding. Its directors could pursue commercial advantage through powers ordinarily held by a state or a monarch, and the company repeatedly used violence to enforce its monopolies, inverting the wise *oikonomia* that keeps the Mono and Poly poles of manifestation in right relation. People whose land and labour entered this arrangement met more than another party to an exchange. They met an enterprise equipped to impose the conditions of exchange, and a company's *oikonomia* became a governing *nomos* over other peoples' lives. In dia-ballein's arithmetic the company counted the whole span of every exchange from its own side, the appropriation `(+1)−(−1)=+2`, its mirror `(−1)−(+1)=−2` entered against the other side: what passed between the parties became what the other side owed. Its navigational, military and administrative knowledge extended that rule, while the account of commercial success measured the undertaking from the position of those who received its returns. Perspective's problem had become imperial: the view from one position was made effective across a world whose other inhabitants were denied an equivalent power to set the terms. Such a power also reproduces itself through custom, rules of succession and managed initiation into its world. In the Netherlands the elite student corps of the older universities, with their initiatory rites, still feed the country's financial and industrial establishment, and the *household* is the key to the pattern: institutions that hold enduring power find ways to "keep it in the family". The Sicilian Mafia is perhaps the example par excellence of this unholy alliance of *nomos*, *oikos* and *nomisma*: it calls itself a family and its business *Cosa Nostra*, "our thing", the household taken as a possession, in the service of an extractive, parasitic and self-concentrating tyranny of the Mono over the Poly it ought to serve.

In the mythemic register these economies have their figures, and reading them belongs to our method: a myth compresses a psychodynamic into persons and deeds, and we unfold the compression back into operations without taking the persons for causes. The fire Prometheus steals passes to a later bearer, *Lucifer*, from *lux* and *ferre*, the light-bearer: the Vulgate's word for the morning star in Isaiah's taunt, "How art thou fallen from heaven, O Lucifer, son of the morning", and the morning star is Venus. Jung observed that "Lucifer, the Morning Star, means Christ as well as the devil", and he spoke without hesitation of "the Luciferian development of science and technology".[^s01-lucifer] One light carries both faces: kept in relation, it is the Logos that discriminates while Eros, Venus, places what has been distinguished into relation; fallen, it keeps the light and loses the love, and becomes a discrimination that will not gather, an economy without its ecology. Gender, in this register, figures polarity within the libido, and dia-ballein draws that polarity exactly: through the slash, `(−1)/(+1)`, the poles keep their harmony, while through `±` one pole can dysregulate the pair, and the long patriarchal dominance of one pole in our laws and economies is that dysregulation made into an order. Genesis sets the presage inside a family when Eve names her first son with a pun on *qanah*, to get or acquire, "I have gotten a man", and Cain, whose name sounds like getting, kills his brother over an offering and then builds the first city.[^s01-cain] What a single life lays down as the Logos matures from infancy into adulthood, a family lays down across generations, which overlap and weave, each born into debts it did not contract and inheritances it did not earn. A counterfeit economy feeds on that weave: it is un-dual where its source is non-dual, a divorce that keeps its parties in a cloying bond; it is un-finite where life is finite, swelling toward the All as growth and imploding toward a precise nought as collapse, without ever reaching Being or non-Being; and it breeds money as false offspring, *tokos*, eating the generations it was meant to feed.[^s01-taylor-antichrist] The `Ø` of the crossed zero is then the mark of missing the mark. *Hamartia*, the missing of the mark whose error we located where a mark is made to stand as though it had abolished its difference from what it marks, shows here its further fault, the proclivity to make the blank page itself into a mark, to stamp the ground as though it were one more coin. Con artists have a word for the person they mean to take, the *mark*, and whoever stamps their own ground becomes the first mark of their own con: the self that mistakes its stamp for its metal, what I have called the **I-Deal**, an ideal dealt as the real, trades the whole psyche for one of its fragments.

What the household, the coin and the chartered company have shown together requires a coherent notion of evil, the question theodicy first put to the definition of God, and Jung lets us state one without hedging. Christian thought long defined evil as a privation of the good, *privatio boni*, and Jung rejected the doctrine because it deprived evil of substance and left the image of wholeness unchallenged: met "on the plane of empirical psychology", evil "is simply the opposite of good".[^s01-privatio] Our account keeps what is true on both sides: a whole's fall into parts is no evil, since differentiation is `0/1` itself; evil begins where a part stops struggling toward the whole and claims to be it, where the `1` plays the `0`. It has no substance of its own, and it is real as an operation, because it does something, to someone, and its consequences can be counted. In the formula §2 develops, the devil is the literalist of the minus sign, reading zero as cancellation where Logos reads it as return.[^s01-devil-minus] Jung found the figure for this operation at the centre of the Christian aeon: the Antichrist "would correspond to the shadow of the self", the counterstroke provoked by the Incarnation, and "the coining of the Antichrist is not just a prophetic prediction—it is an inexorable psychological law", an enantiodromia in which a one-sided light calls up its opposite.[^s01-aion-antichrist] He placed the turn midway between the two fishes of Pisces, where "the time of the Renaissance begins … and with it comes that spirit which culminates in the modern age", the turn at which perspective and the chartered company arose.[^s01-aion-fishes] Nietzsche announced the death of God where economy lives: his madman lights a lantern in the bright morning and runs into the marketplace, where those who already do not believe laugh at him.[^s01-nietzsche-market] The organising power of the God-image did not die there; it passed into the market where its death was proclaimed, so that exchange inherited providence and money became the empty holder, a measure able to stand for anything and therefore the place of the `0` occupied by a `1`; a republic stamped "In God We Trust" on its coins and later printed it on its notes, writing the trust onto the token itself.[^s01-in-god-we-trust] Nietzsche had already traced guilt, *Schuld*, to debt, *Schulden*, and the love of money that the Epistle calls "the root of all evil" is the ego's love for that empty measure, which effaces the Subject and enforces the crossed one.[^s01-schuld] Our present makes this foundation visible as the world dis-organises into an astronomical concentration of wealth, top-heavy as the un-finite always is and exposed to the collapse it courts.[^s01-wealth-gap] The archetype is live in plain view: in 2025 Peter Thiel gave a series of lectures on the Antichrist in San Francisco, drawing on René Girard, who identified Satan, the accuser, with the mechanism by which a crowd unites against a scapegoat, so that naming one's opponents as the Antichrist risks enacting the very accusation it names.[^s01-thiel] §5 takes up the history of this captured power in its own specificity; the economy of mind, like every economy, carries its capacity to fall in the very words through which it holds its value.

Integration is the answer a coherent notion of evil asks for, and Aurobindo practised it under colonial rule.
